-space functor
. Accordingly, given an
hk
-space
X
, we refer to
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
as the
free linear
k
-space
on
X
.

A linear
hk
-space which is not a topological vector space

A linear
hk
-space need not be a topological vector space with respect to the same topology and vector space structure (as mentioned before in
2.2.1.3
):

Example 2.2.2.9
.

Let
X
be an uncountable Tychonov
k
-space. Then the free linear
hk
-space
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
on
X
is
not
a topological vector space.

Proof.

Suppose to the contrary that
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
is a topological vector space. Then
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
is Hausdorff, as the origin is closed in
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
(by the
k
-Hausdorff property). Moreover, in addition to being the free linear
hk
-space on
X
, it is also the free
Hausdorff topological
vector space on
X
, i.e. for every Hausdorff topological vector space
E
and continuous map
f:X\to E
, there is a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{f}:F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)\to E
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. This is because a continuous map from a
k
-space to
E
is equivalently a continuous map to
kE
(which is a linear
hk
-space), so the universal property shown in the diagram does indeed reduce to the one of the free linear
hk
-space.

But this contradicts the result of
[
GM17
, Fact 4.18]
which states that the free Hausdorff topological vector space on an uncountable Tychonov space is never a
k
-space. Therefore,
F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)
is not a topological vector space.
∎

A topological vector space which is not a linear
k
-space

An example in the other direction is the following.

Example 2.2.2.10
.

Let
H
be an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space. Then the weak-
*
dual of
H
is not a
k
-space, see
[
FR72
, Proposition 1]
for a proof.

Remark 2.2.2.11
.

For most purposes, the appropriate substitute for the weak-
*
dual in the context of
k
-spaces seems to be the natural dual
V^{\wedge}=L(V,\mathbb{K})
. When
V
is a Fréchet space, convergence of sequences in
V^{\wedge}
is exactly weak-
*
convergence (
2.2.3.2
). Moreover, still assuming that
V
is a Fréchet space, a subset of
V^{\wedge}
is compact if, and only if, it is compact in the weak-
*
topology (see
2.2.3.4
).

In the introduction, we mentioned that the canonical evaluation pairing between a locally convex Hausdorff topological vector space and its dual is in some sense “unavoidably discontinuous” (see
Section

1.3.4
). In conjunction with the fact that the dual of a Fréchet space is a linear
hk
-space when equipped with the compact-open topology (which we will show later, see
2.2.3.4
), this leads to a further example, showing also that the product in
\mathsf{Top}
does not generally coincide with the one in
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

Proposition 2.2.2.12
.

Let
V
be a locally convex Hausdorff topological vector space (short: LCTVS). Let
V^{\prime}
be its dual, equipped with any topology that turns it into an LCTVS.
Suppose that the map,

\mathsf{ev}:V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}V^{\prime}\to\mathbb{K},\;\;(x,\phi)\mapsto\phi(x),

is continuous (with respect to the product
\times_{\mathsf{Top}}
in
\mathsf{Top}
!).
Then
V
is normable.

Proof.

First, by continuity of
\mathsf{ev}
and local convexity of
V
and
V^{\prime}
, there exist convex neighbourhoods
U_{V}\subseteq V
,
U_{V^{\prime}}\subseteq V^{\prime}
of
0
such that

(2.2)

\mathsf{ev}(U_{V}\times U_{V^{\prime}})\subseteq\{\lambda\in\mathbb{K}\mid|\lambda|\leq 1\}.

This implies that
U_{V}
is weakly bounded, since for every
\phi\in V^{\prime}
, there exists some
t>0
such that
t\phi\in U_{V^{\prime}}
(because
U_{V^{\prime}}
is a neighbourhood of the origin and therefore absorbing), so by (
2.2
)
|\phi(x)|<t^{-1}
, for all
x\in U_{V}
. Hence,
U_{V}
is bounded also in the original topology of
V
(by
[
Rud91
, Theorem 3.18]
) and therefore,
V
is is normable, as it contains a convex bounded neighbourhood of the origin (this is Kolmogorov’s normability criterion
[
Rud91
, Theorem 1.39]
).
∎

Example 2.2.2.13
.

Let
V
be a non-normable Fréchet space. Then the natural dual
V^{\wedge}
agrees with the compact-open dual (
2.2.3.4
), which is an LCTVS. By
2.2.2.12
, the evaluation pairing
V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}V^{\wedge}\to V
is discontinuous, while being continuous when regarded as a map
V\times V\to V
(by cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
). Hence,
V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}V^{\wedge}
is not homeomorphic to its
k
-ification
k(V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}V^{\wedge})=V\times V^{\wedge}
and therefore the LCTVS
V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}V^{\wedge}
is not a
k
-space, illustrating also the difference between the products of
\mathsf{Top}
and
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

2.2.3.
The topology of spaces of continuous linear maps

We now turn to investigating some of the features of the topology of the spaces
L(V,W)
.

For Fréchet spaces, convergence of sequences in
L(V,W)
is strong convergence

What does convergence of sequences in
L(V,W)
amount to? In the case of Fréchet spaces, the answer is particularly simple.

Proposition 2.2.3.1
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space and
W
be a linear
k
-space which is also a Hausdorff topological vector space in the same topology. Then convergence of sequences of continuous linear maps in
L(V,W)
is given equivalently by strong (i.e. pointwise) convergence.

Proof.

The hypotheses that
V
is Fréchet and
W
is a Hausdorff topological vector space ensure that the uniform boundedness principle applies (see
[
Rud91
, p. 42, 2.6 Theorem]
). Hence, a pointwise convergent sequence in
L(V,W)
is equicontinuous and hence also converges compactly and therefore in
L(V,W)
(because the convergent sequences in a topological space and its
k
-ification coincide).
∎

Concerning convergence in the natural dual
V^{\wedge}
of a
Fréchet space
V
, we record the following important
particular case of
2.2.3.1
.

Corollary 2.2.3.2
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space. Then a sequence converges in
V^{\wedge}
if, and only if, it converges pointwise.

For separable Fréchet spaces, the topology on
L(V,W)
is the sequential strong topology

When
V
and
W
are
separable
Fréchet spaces, we can characterise the topology of
L(V,W)
completely in terms of strongly convergent sequences.

Lemma 2.2.3.3
.

Let
V,W
be separable Fréchet spaces. Then
L(V,W)
is a QCB space, and hence in particular sequential space.

Proof.

Since QCB spaces are sequential spaces and separable Fréchet spaces are second countable, this is a special case of
2.2.2.3
.
∎

The natural dual of a Fréchet space is its compact-open dual

In general, the topology on
V^{\wedge}
is
not
the compact-open topology, but only its
k
-ification. However, in case of Fréchet spaces, no
k
-ification is necessary:

Proposition 2.2.3.4
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space. Then
V^{\wedge}
carries the compact-open topology (i.e. the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets). Moreover, the topology of
V^{\wedge}
agrees with the weak-
*
topology on every compact subset of
V^{\wedge}
, and a subset of
V^{\wedge}
is compact if, and only if, it is closed and equicontinuous (which is the case if, and only if it is compact in the weak-
*
topology).

Proof.

Write
V^{*[c.o.]}
for the compact-open dual of
V
. According to the Banach-Dieudonné theorem, the topology on
V^{*[c.o.]}
is the finest topology which agrees with the weak-
*
topology on equicontinuous subsets (see
[
Sch66
, p.151, 6.3 Theorem, Corolary 2]
).
The finest topology which agrees with the weak-
*
topology on equicontinuous subsets is equivalently the finest topology which agrees with the weak-
*
topology on compact subsets of
V^{*[c.o.]}
, since the equicontinuous subsets of
V^{*[c.o.]}
are precisely the relatively compact subsets, by the Arzela-Ascoli theorem (
2.1.5.2
) and the fact that equicontinuous sets of functionals are automatically pointwise bounded
[
Sch66
, p. 83, Corollary]
.
Hence, the Banach-Dieudonné theorem entails that the topology of
V^{*[c.o.]}
is the final topology with respect to all inclusion maps
K\to V^{*[c.o.]}
of compact subsets
K
. In other words,
V^{*[c.o.]}
is the filtered colimit

V^{*[c.o.]}=\colim_{K\subseteq V^{*[c.o.]}}K

over its compact subsets
K
along the inclusion maps. As a colimit of compact Hausdorff spaces,
V^{*[c.o.]}
is a
k
-space and therefore,
V^{\wedge}=kV^{*[c.o.]}=V^{*[c.o.]}
, i.e.
V^{\wedge}
carries the compact-open topology. In conjunction with the aforementioned fact that the compact-open topology agrees with the weak-
*
topology on compact subsets and the Alaoglu-Bourbaki theorem
[
Sch66
, p. 84, 4.3, Corollary]
, this also shows the second part of the claim.
∎

The natural dual of a Fréchet space is a Brauner space

Recall from the introduction that a
Brauner space
is a linear
hk
-space which is hemicompact (a sequential colimit of compact subsets along inclusion maps, see
1.4.3.2
) and a complete locally convex topological vector space with respect to the same underlying topology.

Proof.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space. Then
V^{\wedge}
is a Brauner space.
∎

Proof.

By
2.2.3.4
,
V^{\wedge}
carries the compact-open topology, which is a complete
locally convex vector space topology. It remains to show that
V^{\wedge}
is hemicompact. Since
V
is a Fréchet space, it has a countable base
(U_{n})
of neighbourhoods of the origin. Since

\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}=\{0\},

we have that

\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}U_{n}^{\circ}=\{0\}^{\circ}=V^{\wedge},

where

S^{\circ}:=\{\phi\in W^{\wedge}\mid\forall x\in S:|\phi(x)|\leq 1\}

denotes the
polar
of a subset
S
(see
[
Sch66
, p. 125, 1.3]
). Each
K_{n}:=U_{n}^{\circ}
is compact, so
(K_{n})
is an increasing sequence of compact subsets of
V^{\wedge}
and we claim that

V^{\wedge}=\colim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}

is the sequential colimit (in
k\mathsf{Top}
) along the inclusion maps. By
2.1.4.13
, it suffices to show that every compact subset of
V^{\wedge}
is contained in one of the
K_{n}
. So let
L\subseteq V^{\wedge}
be compact. Then
L
is equicontinuous (see
2.2.3.4
), which implies that there is a basic open neighbourhood
U_{n}
of the origin in
V
such that
L\subseteq U_{n}^{\circ}=K_{n}
, so we are done.
∎

The space
L(V,W)
is a Fréchet space for
V
Brauner and
W
Fréchet

In the other direction, we have the following result.

Proposition 2.2.3.5
.

Let
V
be a Brauner space and let
W
be a Fréchet space. Then
L(V,W)
carries the compact-open topology and is a Fréchet space.

Proof.

By assumption
V
is a Brauner space, so we may write,

V=\colim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n},

where
(K_{n})
is an increasing sequence of compact subsets of
V
. Now, given a countable family of seminorms
(\|\cdot\|_{n}^{W})
inducing the topology on
W
, a countable family of seminorms on
L(V,W)
inducing the compact-open topology is given by

\|f\|_{n,m}^{L(V,W)}:=\sup_{x\in K_{n}}\|f\|_{m}^{V}.\qquad(n,m\in\mathbb{N},\,f\in L(V,W))

Hence, the compact-open topology on
L(V,W)
is a Fréchet topology. In particular, it is metrisable and therefore coincides with the topology on
L(V,W)
.
∎

Let us record a direct corollary.

Corollary 2.2.3.6
.

If
V
is a Brauner space, then
V^{\wedge}
is a Fréchet space and carries the compact-open topology.

The natural dual of a
nuclear
Fréchet space is its strong dual

On the dual of a nuclear Fréchet space (such as the space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of tempered distributions), one usually considers the
strong topology
, which is given by the topology of uniform convergence on bounded subsets (and not to be confused with the strong
operator
topology which is given by the topology of
pointwise
convergence). It turns out that this topology coincides with that of the natural dual.

Recall that a Fréchet space has the
Heine-Borel property
if every closed bounded set of
V
is compact. This always holds in nuclear Fréchet spaces such as
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, see
[
Sch66
, p. 101, Corollary 2]
.

Proposition 2.2.3.7
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space with the Heine-Borel property. Then
V^{\wedge}
carries the strong topology.

Proof.

By
2.2.3.4
, the topology on
V^{\wedge}
is the compact-open topology, i.e. the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets. Since
V
has the Heine-Borel property, uniform convergence on compact subsets is equivalent to uniform convergence on bounded subsets. Hence, the topology on
V^{\wedge}
is the topology of uniform convergence on bounded subsets, i.e. the strong topology.
∎

2.2.4.
Quotients and epimorphisms of linear
k
-Spaces

Epimorphisms of linear
k
-Hausdorff spaces are morphisms with dense image.

We have the following characterisation of epimorphisms in the category of linear
hk
-spaces, which we will need at a later point.

Lemma 2.2.4.1
.

Let
d:V\to W
be a continuous linear map between linear
hk
-spaces
V,W
. Then
d
has dense image if, and only if, it is an epimorphism in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
, i.e. for every pair of maps
f,g:W\to Z
from
W
to some further
k
-Hausdorff linear
k
-space
Z
,
f\circ d=g\circ d
implies
f=g
.

Proof.

For the “if” direction, take
Z=W/\overline{\text{im }d}
to be the quotient of
W
by the closed image of
d
,
f:W\to Z
to be the projection and
g:W\to Z
to be the zero map. Then
f\circ d=0=g\circ d
, and hence, by assumption
f=g=0
. Since
f
is the projection onto
Z=W/\overline{\text{im }d}
this implies that
\overline{\text{im }d}=Z
, so
d
does indeed have dense image.

For the “only if” direction, let
h:=f-g
. Then what we want to show is (equivalently) that
h\circ d=0
implies
h=0
. So suppose that
h\circ d=0
. Then
\text{im }d\subseteq\ker h
. Since
Z
is
k
-Hausdorff,
\ker h=h^{-1}(\{0\})\subseteq W
is closed. Using our assumption that
\overline{\text{im }d}=W
, this implies that

W=\overline{\text{im }d}\subseteq\overline{\ker h}=\ker h\subseteq W.

Therefore,
\ker h=W
and thus,
h=0
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

A direct and useful consequence is:

Lemma 2.2.4.2
.

Let
V,W
be linear
hk
-spaces, let
f:V\to W
be a continuous linear map and suppose that
f
vanishes on some dense linear subspace
D\subseteq V
. Then
f=0
.

Quotients of linear
k
-spaces

The purpose of this section is to define a notion of quotient for linear
k
-spaces and to show that, similar to the case of topological vector spaces, quotient maps of linear
k
-spaces are always open maps (
2.2.4.7
).

Definition 2.2.4.3

(Quotient linear
k
-spaces)
.

Let
V
be a linear
k
-space.

1.

Let
W\subseteq V
be a linear
k
-subspace. We define the quotient of
V
by
W
, written
V/W
, by endowing the (algebraic) quotient vector space with the quotient topology with respect to the projection
V\to V/W
.

2.

A
linear quotient map
is a continuous linear map
p:V\to Z
(with
Z
some linear
k
-space) that is also a (topological) quotient map.

Lemma 2.2.4.4
.

The quotient
V/W
of a linear
k
-space
V
by a linear
k
-subspace
W
is again a linear
k
-space with respect to the induced vector space structure.

Proof.

It is clear that
V/W
is a
k
-space, since (topological) quotients of
k
-spaces are
k
-spaces.

Next, let us show the continuity of

+_{V/W}:V/W\times V/W\to V/W.

W\times W
is contained in the kernel of the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V/W}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces+_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Hence, using the universal property of the (topological) quotient, this composite factors through the projection
V\times V\to(V\times V)/(W\times W)
, yielding the map

f:(V\times V)/(W\times W)\to V/W,\;\;\;(x,y)+W\times W\mapsto x+y+W.

Moreover, the map

i:(V\times V)/(W\times W)\to V/W\times V/W,\;\;\;(x,y)+W\times W\mapsto(x+W,y+W)

is a homeomorphism, since products (in
hk\mathsf{Top}
) of quotients maps are quotient maps are again quotient maps (see
2.1.4.5
. Now, we have that
+_{V/W}=f\circ i^{-1}
and therefore, it is continuous. A similar argument shows the continuity of scalar multiplication.
∎

Linear quotient maps, as introduced in
2.2.4.3
, satisfy the expected universal property:

Lemma 2.2.4.5

(Universal property of linear quotient maps)
.

Let
V,W
be linear
k
-spaces and let
p:V\to W
be a linear quotient map. Suppose that
f:V\to Z
is some further continuous map (with
Z
some linear
k
-space) with the property that
\ker p\subseteq\ker f
, as illustrated in the commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ker p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Then there is a unique map
\overline{f}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ker p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

Proof.

The universal property of quotient maps of topological spaces guarantees the unique existence of
\overline{f}
as a continuous map, while the universal property of surjective linear maps between (algebraic) vector spaces guarantees unique existence of
\overline{f}
as a linear map. Combining both therefore proves the claim.
∎

This also shows that linear quotients maps are always, up to a unique isomorphism, the projection onto some quotient linear
k
-space, as defined in
2.2.4.3
:

Corollary 2.2.4.6
.

Let
p:V\to W
be a linear quotient map. Then there is a unique isomorphism
i
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V/\ker p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Proof.

This follows from applying the universal property of linear quotient maps (
2.2.4.5
) twice, first using that
q
is a linear quotient map and
\ker q\subseteq\ker p
, and then using that
p
is also a linear quotient map and
\ker p\subseteq\ker q
.
∎

One important feature of linear quotient maps that distinguishes them from general quotient maps is that they are always open:

Lemma 2.2.4.7
.

Let
V,W
be linear
k
-spaces and let
p:V\to W
be a surjective continuous linear map. Then
p
is a linear quotient map if, and only if, it is an open map.

Proof.

Open continuous surjective maps are always quotient maps. For the other direction, by
2.2.4.6
, we may assume that
W=V/Z
for some linear
k
-subspace
Z\subseteq V
and that
p:V\to V/Z
is the canonical projection. Now, if
U\subseteq V
is open, then

p(U)=U+W=\bigcup_{w\in W}U+w.

Since translation
(\cdot+x)
is a homeomorphism for any
x\in V
(with continuous inverse
(\cdot-x)
), this shows that
p(U)
is open, as claimed.
∎

2.2.5.
Direct sums and cocompleteness of linear
hk
-spaces

The goal of this section is to construct coproducts of linear
hk
-spaces, which will entail that
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
has all colimits. We will first give a direct construction and then show that this satisfies the required universal property in
2.2.5.3
.

Construction 2.2.5.1

(Direct sum of linear
k
-spaces)
.

1.

For a
finite
family
(V_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
of linear
k
-spaces, define its
direct sum
as the product

\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}.

2.

For an
arbitrary
family
(V_{i})_{i\in I}
of linear
k
-spaces, define its direct sum as the linear
k
-space given by the filtered colimit (in
hk\mathsf{Top}
) over the direct sum of finite subfamilies:

\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}:=\colim_{J\subseteq I\text{ finite}}\bigoplus_{j\in J}V_{j}

Remark 2.2.5.2
.

The underlying set of the direct sum of a family of linear
k
-spaces is the (underlying set of the) algebraic direct sum (since the forgetful functor from
hk\mathsf{Top}
to
\mathsf{Set}
preserves colimits). The addition and scalar multiplication maps obtained this way are continuous, so the direct sum is indeed a linear
k
-space, as suggested.

Proposition 2.2.5.3
.

The direct sum of linear
k
-spaces from
2.2.5.1
is the coproduct in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
.

Proof.

Let
(V_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of linear
k
-spaces. We need to verify that its direct sum verifies the following universal property: for any family of continuous linear maps

f_{i}:V_{i}\to W,\qquad(i\in I),

with
W
some further linear
k
-space, there is a unique continuous linear map

\oplus_{i\in I}f_{i}:\bigoplus_{i\in i}V_{i}\to W,

making the following diagram commute, for each
j\in I
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i\in I}f_{i}}

The unique existence of a linear (not necessarily continuous) such map is guaranteed by the universal property of the direct sum (coproduct) of algebraic vector spaces. Its continuity then follows from the fact that, in order for a map on a filtered colimit limit of
k
-spaces to be continuous, it suffices that its composition with each inclusion is continuous.
∎

Corollary 2.2.5.4
.

{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is a complete and cocomplete pre-abelian category.

Proof.

We have seen that
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is complete (
2.2.2.1
). Cocompleteness follows from the existence of arbitrary coproducts (
2.2.5.3
) and coequalisers of arbitrary pairs
f,g:V\to W
of morphisms (which are given by the cokernel of
f-g
, i.e. the quotient
W/\text{im}(f-g)
). Finally, that
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is a pre-abelian category follows now from the fact that finite products and coproducts coincide, which is how we have constructed them.
∎

2.3.
Smith Duality

An intriguing phenomenon concerning the natural dual space of a Fréchet space is what we refer to as
Smith duality
, first observed in the context of Banach spaces by Smith in
[
Smi52
]
. Let us write

V^{\wedge\wedge}:=(V^{\wedge})^{\wedge}

for the “natural double dual” of a linear
k
-space
V
. We then have:

Theorem 2.3.0.1

(Smith Duality Theorem)
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet space,
or
a Brauner space. Then the canonical evaluation map

\eta:V\to V^{\wedge\wedge},\;\;\;x\mapsto(\phi\mapsto\phi(x))

is an isomorphism (i.e. a linear homeomorphism).

Proof.

That
\eta
is continuous is a consequence of cartesian closure of
k\mathsf{Top}
. By
2.2.3.4
and
2.2.3.6
,
V^{\wedge}
carries the compact-open topology which, by completeness of
V
, also coincides with the topology of uniform convergence on absolutely convex compact subsets (since, in complete spaces, the closed absolutely convex hull of a compact set is compact). That
\eta
is a bijection then follows from the Mackey-Arens theorem (for a statement of the Mackey-Arens and the necessary preliminaries, see
[
Sch66
, p. 131, 3.2 Theorem]
).

It remains to show that
\eta
is also an open map. Let
U\subseteq V
be an open neighbourhood of the origin. Since
V
is locally convex, we may suppose that
U
is closed and absolutely convex (meaning that ). Then the
polar
of
U
,

U^{\circ}:=\{\phi\in V^{\wedge}\mid\forall x\in U:|\phi(x)|\leq 1\},

is equicontinuous and hence compact (by the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem). Now, the
double polar
(or,
bipolar
),

(U^{\circ})^{\circ}:=\{\chi\in V^{\wedge\wedge}\mid\forall\phi\in U^{\circ}:|\chi(\phi)|\leq 1\},

is a neighbourhood of the origin in the compact-open topology of
V^{\wedge\wedge}
and therefore in
V^{\wedge\wedge}
. The
bipolar theorem
(see
[
Sch66
, p. 126, 1.5 Theorem]
) now says precisely that

(U^{\circ})^{\circ}=\eta(U),

so the image
\eta(U)
of
U
under
\eta
is open. Since
U
was arbitrary,
\eta
is an open map, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Remark 2.3.0.2
.

The original statement and proof of the duality between Fréchet and Brauner spaces is the subject of
[
Bra
]
. Our proof is essentially an adaption of the original proof of the Banach space case in
[
Smi52
]
(which can even be adapted to classes of locally convex topological vector spaces more general than Fréchet spaces, see, for example,
[
Wat68
]
).

Remark 2.3.0.3
.

In light of
Remark

2.1.6.3
and
2.2.2.3
, when
V
is a separable, even if we had chosen to work over a different (reasonable) category of topological spaces,
V^{\wedge}
would still carry the
same topology
. In other words, for separable Fréchet spaces, Smith duality does not depend on the choice of base category. From this perspective, Smith duality is a particularly natural phenomenon.

Remark 2.3.0.4
.

An informal interpretation of the Smith Duality Theorem might suggest the paradoxical consequence that “all Banach spaces are reflexive” (paradoxical because Banach spaces are typically not reflexive, in the established sense of the word “reflexive”). This is no contradiction, of course, as the topology on the natural dual of a Banach space (in the sense of
2.2.2.4
) is weaker than the norm topology (and always strictly so in the infinite-dimensional case).

Remark 2.3.0.5
.

The proof of
2.3.0.1
uses a large part of the canon of theorems of locally convex functional analysis: the uniform bounded principle and the Banach-Dieudonné theorem (both in form of
2.2.3.4
), as well as the Mackey-Arens theorem (which in turn uses the Alaoglu and Hahn-Banach theorems). Considering that the formulation of
2.3.0.1
takes the perspective of linear
k
-spaces, this seems like a detour, and raises the question of whether there is a proof more close to the language of linear
k
-spaces. While we will not pursue this question here, we remark that a result in this direction is the proof of (the Banach space case of) Smith duality in the language of
condensed mathematics

[
Sch19
, p. 21, Theorem 4.2]
.

Remark 2.3.0.6
.

As a final remark, we point out that Smith duality is strongly related to, and can be viewed as an instance of,
Pontryagin duality
. This was Smith’s original motivation (see
[
Smi52
]
for any further details).

As an (anti-)equivalence of categories

We can also formulate Smith duality as an equivalence of categories.

Corollary 2.3.0.7
.

The natural dual space functor,

(-)^{\wedge}:\mathsf{Fre}^{\text{op}}\to\mathsf{Bra},\;\;V\mapsto V^{\wedge},\;f\mapsto f^{\wedge},

where
\mathsf{Fre}
and
\mathsf{Bra}
are the full subcategories of
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
spanned by Smith and Brauner spaces, respectively, induces an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

This follows directly from
2.3.0.1
together with the fact that
\eta
defines two natural transformations,

\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{Fre^{\text{op}}}}\to(-)^{\wedge\wedge},\;\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{Bra}}\to(-)^{\wedge\wedge}.

(The proof of naturality of
\eta
is the same as for algebraic vector spaces.)
∎

Chapter 3
Monads, Tensor Products and Duality

This chapter is divided into three parts. The first is a brief introduction to monads, including a number of examples specific to our cause. The second part,
Section

3.2
, will similarly deal with the basics of the theory of (closed, symmetric) monoidal categories. All this is done in service to the third part,
Section

3.3
, where we will introduce
separated objects
, a general notion of an object “embedded into its double dual”. We show how this is related to the
extensional-separated Chu construction
of
[
Bar98
]
and apply both notions to the category
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of linear
hk
-spaces. The separated objects in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
are the
replete
linear
hk
-spaces, and the separated-extensional Chu construction leads to the notion of
paired linear
hk
-space
.

While the first two sections of this Chapter have a purely expository purpose, the developments concerning the
separated objects
of
Section

3.3.2
are original, as is the relation to the separated-extensional Chu construction given in
3.3.4.11
, the construction of free objects for the latter category (see
3.3.5.9
), and the application of these results to
hk
-spaces. This being said, the results of
Section

3.3.2
concerning
separated objects
can be seen as special cases of more general results related to the
idempotent monad associated to an arbitrary monad
, applied to the
double dualisation monad
, see
[
Fak70
]
as well as
[
LW14
]
for a generalisation and an overview of related results, with functional-analytic examples. However, we do not use these general results, significantly simplifying the development of these ideas for the particular case that we are interested in, thus making it more accessible and streamlined to our cause.

3.1.
Monads

3.1.1.
What is a monad?

Conceptually, there are at least two points of view on what a monad is. According to the first, monads provide a “consistent way of extending spaces to include [generalised] elements and [generalised] functions of a specific kind”
[
Per19
, p. 132]
.
In this way, they allow us to model (various sorts of) non-determinism, as illustrated by the example of probability monads, mentioned in the introduction (see
Section

1.3.2
, as well as
[
Per19
, Section 5.1]
for a detailed explanation). A second perspective is that monads are “generalised algebraic theories”, the reason for which should become apparent by the end of this section (see also
[
Per19
, Section 5.2]
for an elementary exposition). With these ideas in mind, we turn to the definition:

Definition 3.1.1.1

(Monad)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category. A
monad

(T,i,m)
on
\mathsf{C}
is

1.

an endofunctor,

T:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C},

together with

2.

two natural transformations, the
unit
,

i:\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{C}}\to T,

and the
multiplication
,

m:T\circ T\to T,

such that

3.

the
monad laws
hold, meaning that following two diagrams, the
unit law
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\;\;\;m^{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(i^{X})\>\>\>}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{TX}\;}

and the
associativity law
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTTX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TTX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{TTX}\;\;\;\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(m^{TX})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{TX}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\;\;\;m^{TX}}

commute, where
X
is any object from
\mathsf{C}
and
m^{TY}:TTY\to TY
,
i^{Y}:Y\to TY
are the components at the object
Y
of the natural transformations
m,i
.

Remark 3.1.1.2
.

The reason for the terminology “unit” and “multiplication” is that monads can be considered a special case of the concept of a monoid in a monoidal category (see
[
Rie17
, p. 154]
). The unit and associativity laws are precisely the corresponding laws for monoid, under this interpretation.

3.1.2.
Examples of monads

Adjunctions induce monads

From an adjunction one always obtains a monad in the following way.

Example 3.1.2.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}
be categories and suppose that the functor

F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}

is left adjoint to

G:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C},

i.e. we have a natural isomorphism

\phi^{X,Y}:\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(FX,Y)\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(X,GY).\qquad(X\in\mathsf{C},Y\in\mathsf{D})

From this, we obtain two natural transformations,

i:=\phi^{X,FX}(\mathsf{id}_{FX}):X\to GFX,\qquad(X\in\mathsf{C})

and,

m:=G((\phi^{GFX,FX})^{-1}(\mathsf{id}_{GFX})):GFGFX\to GFX.\qquad(X\in\mathsf{C})

Then
(G\circ F,i,m)
is a monad on
\mathsf{C}
(see
[
ML71
, p. 138f]
).

Remark 3.1.2.2
.

As we will see,
every
monad arises from an adjunction in this way (see
Section

3.1.3
).

Monads induced from free-forgetful adjunctions

The following example is conceptually important, illustrating the role that monads play in the category-theoretic approach to universal algebra (which in turn motivates the definition of the Eilenberg-Moore category,
see
Remark

3.1.3.2
).

Example 3.1.2.3

(Set-of-terms monads)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be some category of algebraic structures, such as the category
\mathsf{Mon}
of monoids, the category
\mathsf{Grp}
of groups, the category
\mathsf{Ring}
of rings, or the category
R\mathsf{Mod}
of
R
-modules over some ring
R
. (The notion of “category of algebraic stuctures” can be made precise using the language of
Lawvere theories
, see
[
HP07
]
.) In any such case, the forgetful functor
|\cdot|:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{Set}
has a left adjoint
T_{\mathsf{A}}
(the “free
\mathsf{A}
-object on a set”). Given a set
X
,
|T_{\mathsf{A}}(X)|
may be thought of as the set of terms built freely from elements of
X
(serving as “abstract variables”) and the given algebraic operations. For example,
|T_{\mathsf{Mon}}(X)|
is the free monoid on
X
(equivalently, the set of lists with entries from
X
, or the set of
strings
from the
alphabet

X
);
|T_{\mathsf{Grp}}(X)|
is the set of (group theoretic)
words
on
X
(i.e. the free group on
X
);
|T_{\mathsf{Ring}}(X)|\cong\mathbb{Z}[X]
is the polynomial ring on
X
over
\mathbb{Z}
; and
|T_{R\mathsf{Mod}}(X)|
is the set of finite formal
R
-linear combinations of elements of
X
.

Since the endofunctor
|T_{\mathsf{A}}(-)|:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set}
is the composite of a functor with its left adjoint, by
Example

3.1.2.1
, it carries the structure of a monad, the
set-of-terms
monad associated to
\mathsf{A}
. In this case, the unit corresponds to the inclusion
X\to|T_{\mathsf{A}}(X)|
of single-variable terms, and the multiplication
|T_{\mathsf{A}}(|T_{\mathsf{A}}(X)|)|\to|T_{\mathsf{A}}(X)|
corresponds to a formal application of the given algebraic operations to terms.

The next example will play a crucial role later on. For example, it will be used to show the existence of a good notion of tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces.

Example 3.1.2.4

(The free-linear-
hk
-space monad)
.

By
2.2.2.7
, the forgetful functor

{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}\to hk\mathsf{Top}

has a left adjoint, the free-linear-
hk
-space functor,

F_{\mathbb{K}}:hk\mathsf{Top}\to{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}.

The monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}
arising from this adjunction will, by a very slight abuse of notation, also be denoted by
F_{\mathbb{K}}
.

A final example of a monad arising from a free-forgetful adjunction demonstrates that monads also come up naturally in situations which one might not
a priori
judge as being of an algebraic nature.

Example 3.1.2.5

(Ultrafilter monad)
.

Let
|-|:\mathsf{CompHaus}\to\mathsf{Set}
be the forgetful functor from the category of compact Hausdorff spaces to the category of sets. This functor has a left adjoint (the
Stone-Čech compactification
), yielding a monad with underlying endofunctor

\beta:\mathsf{Set}\to\mathsf{Set},

such that
\beta X
(with
X
any set) can be identified with the set of ultrafilters on
X
(see
[
ML71
, p. 157, section 9]
).

Probability monads

As mentioned in the introduction, probability monads, being both rich in structure and applications, are one of our motivations for studying interactions between measure theory, functional analysis and monads (see also
Section

1.3.2
of the introduction).

Example 3.1.2.6

(The Giry monad on Polish spaces)
.

The Giry monad on the category
\mathsf{Pol}
of Polish (i.e. separable, completely metrisable) spaces (with continuous maps as morphisms) provides an example of a probability monad (see
Example

1.3.2.2
for details).

Example 3.1.2.7

(Radon monad,
[
Swi74
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{CompHaus}
be the category of compact Hausdorff spaces. For a compact Hausdorff space
X
, let
\mathcal{P}_{r}(X)
be the space of Radon probability measures on
X
, with the topology of weak convergence (which is a compact Hausdorff topology). Then the resulting functor

\mathcal{P}_{r}:\mathsf{CompHaus}\to\mathsf{CompHaus}

may be equipped with the structure of a monad (in exactly the same way as for the Giry monad on
\mathsf{Pol}
, see
[
Kei08
, Proposition 2.5]
for a detailed construction).

In Chapter 4, we will construct a probability monad
\mathcal{P}
on
hk\mathsf{Top}
which restricts to both the Giry monad on Polish spaces and the Radon monad on compact Hausdorff spaces, thus in some sense interpolating between the two.

3.1.3.
Modules over a monad (the Eilenberg-Moore category)

In the interpretation of monads as “generalised algebraic theories”, the
Eilenberg-Moore category
is the category of “generalised algebraic structures” that the given monad describes.

Definition 3.1.3.1

(Modules over a monad, Eilenberg-Moore category)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category and let
(T,i,m)
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}
.

1.

A
T
-module is an object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
together with a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism (“structure morphism”),

t^{A}:TA\to A,

such that the following two diagrams,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TTA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(t^{A})\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}}

commute.

2.

Let
A
and
B
be
T
-modules (with structure maps
t^{A},t^{B}
, which we will subsequently sometimes leave implicit). A
morphism of
T
-modules
is a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism

f:A\to B

such that

f\circ t^{A}=t^{B}\circ Tf,

i.e. the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TB}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{B}}

3.

We denote the category of
T
-modules (with morphisms of
T
-modules as morphisms), also called the
Eilenberg-Moore category
of
T
, by
\mathsf{C}^{T}
.

Remark 3.1.3.2
.

T
-modules are also known as “algebras for the monad
T\>
” or simply “
T
-algebras”. This terminology comes from the usage of monads for the purpose of universal algebra. In this context, the term “algebra” is not reserved for “algebras over a field (or ring)”, but is used for any kind of algebraic structure, so viewing monads as generalised algebraic theories renders this terminology quite coherent. However, since vector space play a dominant role in our context (i.e. our context is closer to
linear
as opposed to universal algebra), the term “algebra” referring to something other than an algebra over a field or ring may be confusing, which is why we will adhere to the name “
T
-module”.

Examples of categories of modules over a monad.

Modules over a monad generalise many common kinds of mathematical structures. Let us see what the Eilenberg-Moore category of some of the exemplary monads given in
Section

3.1.2
is.

1.

If
T:=|T_{\mathsf{A}}(-)|
is a the set-of-terms monad associated to category
\mathsf{A}
of algebraic structures (see
Example

3.1.2.3
), a
T
-module is equivalently just an object of
\mathsf{A}
: the Eilenberg-Moore category of
T
is canonically equivalent to
\mathsf{A}
itself. For example, a module over the free-group monad on
\mathsf{Set}
is equivalently a group, and similarly, a module over the free-commutative-ring monad
\mathbb{Z}[-]
is equivalently a commutative ring.

2.

Analogously to the previous point, an
F_{\mathbb{K}}
-module (where
F_{\mathbb{K}}
is the free-linear-
hk
-space monad) is equivalently a linear
hk
-space.

3.

A module over the ultrafilter monad
\beta
on
\mathsf{Set}
is equivalently a compact Hausdorff space (see
[
ML71
, p. 157, Section 9, Theorem 1]
). This explains why compact Hausdorff spaces behave categorically a lot like algebraic structures (e.g. in that a morphism of compact Hausdorff spaces is an isomorphism if, and only if, it is bijective).

4.

Modules over the Radon monad are equivalently compact convex subsets of locally convex Hausdorff topological vector spaces, with morphisms of modules corresponding to affine maps between compact convex sets (this is
[
Swi74
, Theorem 3]
).

The free module over a monad.

It turns out that
every
monad arises from a “free-forgetful” adjunction as described in
Example

3.1.2.1
. To see this, we first observe that
TX
always admits an obvious
T
-module structure.

Definition 3.1.3.3

(Free
T
-module)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category and let
(T,i,m)
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}
. Given an object
X
of
\mathsf{C}
, endow
TX
with the structure of a
T
-module by defining the structure map,

t^{TX}:TTX\to TX,

to be
t^{TX}:=m
(the multiplication). (That this does indeed define a
T
-module follows directly from the monad laws.) With this
T
-module structure, we call
TX
the
free
T
-module on
X
.

The name “free
T
-module” is justified by the following universal property, which also shows the aforementioned claim that every monad arises from a free-forgetful adjunction.

Proposition 3.1.3.4

(Universal property of free
T
-modules)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category, let
(T,i,m)
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}
and let
X
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. Then for any
\mathsf{C}
-morphism,

f:X\to A,

from
X
to some
T
-module
A
, there is a unique morphism of
T
-modules,

\overline{f}:TX\to A,

such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

In other words, we have an adjunction,

\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(X,|A|)\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}^{T}}(TX,A),\qquad(X\in\mathsf{C},A\in\mathsf{C}^{T})

where
|-|:\mathsf{C}^{T}\to\mathsf{C}
denotes the forgetful functor.

Proof.

Take
\overline{f}:=t^{A}\circ Tf
. This makes the desired diagram commute and thus shows existence. For uniqueness, notice that
\overline{f}
is necessarily of this form: being a morphism of
T
-modules,

\overline{f}\circ m=t^{A}\circ T\overline{f}.

But then,

\overline{f}\circ m\circ Ti=t^{A}\circ T\overline{f}\circ Ti.

Using the unit law, the left hand side is just
\overline{f}
, while the right hand side equals
t^{A}\circ Tf
, by functoriality of
T
and commutativity of the diagram. Thus,
\overline{f}:=t^{A}\circ Tf
, showing uniqueness.
∎

3.2.
Tensor Products: Monoidal Categories

What does it mean for a category to admit a “good notion of tensor product”? And how does one construct such tensor products? Questions like these are naturally treated using the language of
monoidal categories
(and their relatives).

3.2.1.
Monoidal categories

Definition 3.2.1.1
.

A
monoidal category
is a category
\mathsf{C}
together with

1.

a functor,

\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}:\mathsf{C}\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C},

called the
tensor product
,

2.

an object
I_{\mathsf{C}}
, called the
tensor unit
,

3.

a natural isomorphism,

\alpha^{A,B,C}:(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C\cong A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C),\;\;\;\;(A,B,C\in\mathsf{C}

called the
associator
,

4.

two further natural isomorphisms,

\lambda^{A}:I_{\mathsf{C}}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A\cong A,\;\;\rho^{A}:A\otimes{\mathsf{C}}I_{\mathsf{C}}\cong A,\;\;\;(A\in\mathsf{C})

called the left and right
unitors
, respectively,

subject to the commutativity of the following two diagrams, the
coherence conditions
:

1.

The triangle identity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}I_{\mathsf{C}})\otimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(I_{\mathsf{C}}\otimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{A,I_{\mathsf{C}},B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{A}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}I_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{A}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\lambda_{B}}

2.

The pentagon identity:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(C\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(((A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(C\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}(B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C))\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}((B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B,C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{A,B,C\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{A,B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}C,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{A,B,C}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\alpha^{B,C,D}}

Warning 3.2.1.2
.

Despite being a crucial part of the structure of a monoidal category, we will sometimes leave the associator and unitors implicit, as they are often easily inferred from context. For example, we will use (common) phrases like “let
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,I)
be a monoidal category”, specifying the associator and unitors only when they become relevant. This is (partially) justified by
Mac Lane’s Coherence Theorem
(see
[
ML71
, p. 165-170]
) which is also the motivation for the otherwise perhaps somewhat obscure coherence conditions in the definition of a monoidal category.

Example 3.2.1.3
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category with finite products. Then
\mathsf{C}
is a monoidal category with respect to the cartesian product
\times
as the tensor product, the initial object
1
as tensor unit, and the associator and unitors given in the evident way.

Example 3.2.1.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and let
R\mathsf{Mod}
be the category of modules over
R
. Then
R\mathsf{Mod}
becomes a monoidal category under the tensor product
\otimes_{R}
of
R
-modules, with
R
serving as the tensor unit. (As before, we omit an explicit description of the associator and unitors.) This example illustrates that monoidal
structure
is not a mere
property
of a category: the category of
R\mathsf{Mod}
is also a monoidal category with respect to the cartesian product.

Monoidal functors and adjunctions

Monoidal categories come with several corresponding notions of
monoidal functor
, varying in strength.

Definition 3.2.1.5

(lax/strong/strict monoidal functors)
.

A
lax monoidal functor
between monoidal categories
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
is a functor
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
together with

1.

a morphism, the
unit
,

I_{\mathsf{D}}\to F(I_{\mathsf{C}}),

2.

and a natural transformation, the
multiplication

F(A)\otimes F(B)\to F(A\otimes B),\;\;\;\;(A,B\in\mathsf{C})

subject to a number of coherence conditions (which we omit, see
[
ML71
, pp. 255-56]
for a detailed definition). If unit and multiplication are both isomorphisms, then
F
is a
strong monoidal functor
, and if, in addition, these isomorphisms are even
identities
, then
F
is called a
strict monoidal functor
. Even though a monoidal functor is a functor with additional
structure
, we will, as is common, sometimes abuse terminology and treat this structure as though it were a mere
property
. For example, when we say that a functor
F
“is” a monoidal functor, we mean that
F
admits a specific structure of a monoidal functor which is left implicit.

Adjunctions also have a monoidal counterpart.

Definition 3.2.1.6
.

A
monoidal adjunction
is an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

between monoidal categories
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}
in which the left adjoint
F
is a strong monoidal functor.

3.2.2.
Symmetric monoidal categories

Demanding further structure which renders the tensor product commutative, or
symmetric
, in a suitable sense, leads to the following definition.

Definition 3.2.2.1
.

A symmetric monoidal category is a monoidal category
\mathsf{C}
together with a natural isomorphism

\sigma^{A,B}:A\otimes B\cong B\otimes A,\;\;\;\;(A,B\in\mathsf{C})

called the
braiding
, satisfying the identity

\sigma^{B,A}\circ\sigma^{A,B}=\mathsf{id}_{A\otimes B},

as well as certain coherence conditions (which we omit, a detailed definition can be found in
[
ML71
, pp. 252-53]
).

Symmetric monoidal functors and adjunctions

As for the case of bare monoidal categories, there are associated notions of
symmetric monoidal functor
and
symmetric monoidal adjunction
. We will only need the case of
strong
symmetric monoidal functors.

Definition 3.2.2.2
.

A
strong symmetric monoidal functor
is a strong monoidal functor
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
between symmetric monoidal categories, which is compatible with the braiding
\sigma
on both categories in that the following diagram commutes for all
A,B\in\mathsf{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FA\otimes FB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{FB\otimes FA}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(A\otimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(B\otimes A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\sigma)}

Definition 3.2.2.3
.

A
symmetric monoidal adjunction
is a monoidal adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

between
symmetric
monoidal categories
\mathsf{C},\mathsf{D}
in which the left adjoint
F
is a strong
symmetric
monoidal functor.

3.2.3.
Symmetric monoidal monads: commutative monads

Adapting the notion of monad to the setting of symmetric monoidal categories leads to the notion of
symmetric monoidal monad
. We will also use the term
commutative monad
as a synonym for “symmetric monoidal monad”. (Despite the definition of “commutative monad” usually being slightly different, they are equivalent concepts, see
[
Koc72
]
.)

Definition 3.2.3.1

(Commutative Monad)
.

Let
(C,\boxtimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
symmetric monoidal monad
, or
commutative monad
,
(T,i,m,\otimes)
on
C
is

1.

a monad
(T,i,m)
on
C
, together with

2.

a natural transformation,

\otimes^{X,Y}:TX\boxtimes TY\to T(X\boxtimes Y),\qquad(X,Y\in\mathsf{C})

such that

3.

(T,i,\otimes)
is a monoidal functor, satisfying two further coherence conditions which we omit (see
[
Sea12
, 1.2]
for the complete definition),

4.

such that the following diagram commutes, where
\sigma
is the braiding (see
3.2.2.1
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TX\boxtimes TY}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TY\boxtimes TX}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(X\boxtimes Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(Y\boxtimes X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}

Example 3.2.3.2
.

The free-
R
-module monad
R^{(-)}
becomes a commutative monad on
(\mathsf{Set},\times,1)
if we let

\otimes:R^{(X)}\times R^{(Y)}\to R^{(X\times Y)},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(X,Y\in\mathsf{Set})

be the map defined by sending a pair of basis element
(x,y)\in R^{(X)}\times R^{(Y)}
to the basis element
(x,y)\in R^{(X\times Y)}
and using the universal property of free
R
-modules to extend by linearity.

Example 3.2.3.3
.

The free-linear-
hk
-space becomes a commutative monad on
(hk\mathsf{Top},\times,1)
, in the same way as the free-
R
-module monad,
mutatis mutandis
(using the universal property of free linear
hk
-spaces in place of the one for free
R
-modules).

Remark 3.2.3.4
.

One can generalise the previous two examples to the case of
R
-module objects internal to a sufficiently well-behaved cartesian closed category, and even further to the case of modules over
commutative algebraic theories
in place of
R
-modules, see
[
Low12
]
.

Binary Morphisms and tensor products of modules over commutative monads.

The following is a generalisation of the notion of a bilinear map of modules over a ring to the case of modules over a commutative monad:

Definition 3.2.3.5

(Binary morphism,
[
Sea12
]
, 2.1)
.

Let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\boxtimes,I)
. A
binary morphism
of
T
-modules is a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism
f:A\boxtimes B\to C
(where
A,B,C
are
T
-modules) such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA\boxtimes TB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}\,\boxtimes\,t^{B}\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{C}}

Remark 3.2.3.6
.

In
[
Sea12
]
, binary morphisms of
T
-modules are referred to as “bimorphisms”. This term is however slightly ambiguous, as it is also sometimes used for morphisms which are both an epimorphism and a monomorphism.

The above definition of binary morphism may seem somewhat obscure. We can shed some light on it by introducing a notion of “morphism in one variable” and then showing that a binary morphism is nothing but a “morphism in
both
variables”.

Definition 3.2.3.7

(Morphism in a variable)
.

Let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\boxtimes,I)
, let
A,B,C
be
T
-modules and let
f:A\boxtimes B\to C
be a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism.

1.

f
is a
morphism of
T
-modules in the left variable
if, and only if, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{TA\boxtimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\circ(\mathsf{id}_{TA}\boxtimes i^{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{id}_{B}\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{C}}

commutes.

2.

f
is a
morphism of
T
-modules in the right variable
if, and only if, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes TB}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TC}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes\circ(i^{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{id}_{TB})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{A}\boxtimes t^{B}\;}

commutes.

If this definition is still not quite transparent, considering the case when
\boxtimes
is an ordinary cartesian product should clear it up:

Lemma 3.2.3.8
.

Let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a concrete category
\mathsf{C}
with concrete finite products (i.e.
\mathsf{C}
is equipped with a “forgetful” faithful functor
\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
, which we will leave implicit, preserving finite products). Then a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism
f:A\times B\to C
is a morphism of
T
-modules in the left (right, resp.) variable if, and only if, for any
b_{0}\in B
(for any
a_{0}\in A
, resp.), the map

a\mapsto f(a,b_{0}),\;\;\;(b\mapsto f(a_{0},b),\text{ resp.})

is a morphism of
T
-modules.

Proof.

Because of the symmetry of the situation, we consider only the case of a morphism of
T
-modules in the left variable. For the “if” direction, suppose that for all
b_{0}\in B
,
a\mapsto f(a,b_{0})
is a morphism of
T
-modules. Then for all
b_{0}\in B
,
\alpha\in TA
,

\displaystyle f\circ(t^{A}\times\mathsf{id}_{B})(\alpha,b_{0})

\displaystyle=f(t^{A}(\alpha),b_{0})

\displaystyle=t^{C}(\,T(f(-,b_{0}))(\alpha)\,)

\displaystyle=t^{C}(\,T(f)(\alpha\otimes i^{B}(b_{0}))\,)

\displaystyle=t^{C}\circ Tf\circ\otimes\circ(\mathsf{id}_{TA}\times i^{B})(\alpha,b_{0}).

This shows that
f
is a morphism of
T
-modules in the left variable.

For the “only if” direction, suppose that
f
is a morphism of
T
-modules in the left variable and let
b_{0}\in B
. Then

\displaystyle f(t^{A}(\alpha),b_{0})

\displaystyle=f\circ(t^{A}\times\mathsf{id}_{B})(\alpha,b_{0})

\displaystyle=t^{C}\circ Tf\circ\otimes\circ(\mathsf{id}_{TA}\times i^{B})(\alpha,b_{0})

\displaystyle=t^{C}(\,T(f)(\alpha\otimes i^{B}(b_{0}))\,)

\displaystyle=t^{C}(\,T(f(-,b_{0}))(\alpha)\,)

where we have used that
f
is a morphism of
T
-modules in the left variable in going from the first to the second line. This shows that
f(-,b_{0})
is a morphism of
T
-modules, completing the proof.
∎

We can now give a three equivalent characterisations of binary morphisms. The second confirms the intuition that a binary morphism is indeed a “morphism in both variables”, while the third hints on how to construct the
tensor product of
T
-modules
.

Proposition 3.2.3.9

(Characterisations of binary morphisms)
.

As before, let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\boxtimes,I)
, let
A,B,C
be
T
-modules and let
f:A\boxtimes B\to C
be a
\mathsf{C}
-morphism. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is a binary morphism of
T
-modules.

2.

f
is both a morphism of
T
-modules in left variable and a morphism of
T
-modules in the right variable.

3.

The diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(TA\boxtimes TB)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{T(A\times B)}\circ\,{T(\otimes)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(t^{A}\boxtimes t^{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{C}\circ\,Tf}

commutes.

Proof.

The equivalence of the first two points is
[
Sea12
, Proposition 2.1.2]
; the equivalence of the first and the last item is
[
Sea12
, Lemma 2.3.2]

∎

The tensor product of
T
-modules

The following is a generalisation of the tensor product of modules over a commutative ring to the setting of commutative monads.

Definition 3.2.3.10

(Tensor product of
T
-modules)
.

Let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\boxtimes,I)
. The
tensor product

A\otimes_{T}B
of two
T
-modules
A
and
B
is defined, if it exists, as the co-equaliser (in
\mathsf{M}
) of the two morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(TA\boxtimes TB)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\otimes)\circ t^{\,TA\boxtimes TB}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(t^{A}\boxtimes t^{B})}

This construction satisfies the expected universal property
[
Sea12
, Lemma 2.3.3]
:

Proposition 3.2.3.11

(Universal property of tensor product)
.

As before, let
(T,i,m,\otimes)
be a commutative monad over a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathsf{C},\boxtimes,I)
and let
A,B
be objects of
\mathsf{C}
and suppose that the tensor product
A\otimes B
exists. Then, for every binary morphism of
T
-modules,

f:A\boxtimes B\to C,

to some object
C
of
\mathsf{C}
, there is a unique morphism of
T
-modules,

\overline{f}:A\otimes B\to C,

such that the diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commutes, where the map
A\boxtimes B\to A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B
is the composite of the maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\boxtimes B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T(A\boxtimes B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{M}B,}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

with
q
the canonical map to the coequaliser.

The category of modules over a symmetric monoidal monad is now itself symmetric monoidal
[
Sea12
, Theorem 2.2.5]
:

Theorem 3.2.3.12
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
a symmetric monoidal category, let
T
be a commutative monad on
\mathsf{C}
and suppose that the Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathsf{C}^{T}
has all coequalisers. Then the tensor product of
T
-modules endows
\mathsf{C}^{T}
with a symmetric monoidal structure.

Example 3.2.3.13

(Tensor product of
R
-modules)
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring and consider the category the free-
R
-module monad
R^{(-)}
(which is a commutative monad on
(\mathsf{Set},\times,1)
). As discussed before, the category of
R^{(-)}
-modules is then equivalently the category of
R
-modules. In this case, a binary morphism is precisely a bilinear map (this is implied by
3.2.3.8
) and then it follows from the respective universal properties that the tensor product of
R^{(-)}
-modules agrees with the usual tensor product.

Example 3.2.3.14

(Tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces)
.

Since linear
hk
-spaces are equivalently
F_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules (where
F_{\mathbb{K}}
is the free-linear-
hk
-space monad, a commutative monad),
3.2.3.10
yields a natural notion of tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces. We denote this tensor product by
\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
. By
3.2.3.12
, we conclude that the category
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of linear
hk
-spaces becomes a symmetric monoidal category under the tensor product
\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
.

As we will see,
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is even a
closed symmetric monoidal category
.

3.2.4.
Closed symmetric monoidal categories

Definition 3.2.4.1
.

A
closed symmetric monoidal category
is a symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{C}
, equipped with an
internal hom
functor,

[-,-]_{\mathsf{C}}:\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C},

and natural isomorphisms

\tau^{A,B,C}:\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B,C)\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A,[B,C]_{\mathsf{C}}),\qquad(A,C\in\mathsf{C})

exhibiting
-\otimes B
as left adjoint to
[B,-]_{\mathsf{C}}
, for every object
B\in\mathsf{C}
. The adjunction
-\otimes B\dashv[B,-]_{\mathsf{C}}
is called the
tensor-hom adjunction
. If unambiguous, we drop indices and simply write
[-,-]
instead of
[-,-]_{\mathsf{C}}
.

Remark 3.2.4.2
.

Since adjoints are essentially unique, the internal hom of a closed symmetric monoidal category is already essentially determined by its monoidal structure. In light of this, a
closed structure
on a symmetric monoidal category is “property-like”.

Remark 3.2.4.3
.

In
[
Bar91
]
(as well as other writings of Barr), closed symmetric monoidal category are referred to “autonomous categories” (not to be confused with
*
-autonomous
categories, which are a particular sort of symmetric monoidal category, see
3.3.3.1
).

Remark 3.2.4.4

(Relation to “external” hom functor)
.

Note that, by the tensor-hom adjunction,

\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(I,[-,-,])\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(I\otimes-,-)\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(-,-),

where
I
is the tensor unit. Hence, a “global element”
I\to[-,-]
of the internal hom corresponds bijectively to an element of the “external” hom
\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(-,-)
.

Examples of closed symmetric monoidal categories

When the tensor product is the cartesian product
\times
, the notion of symmetric monoidal category reduces to that of a cartesian closed category. In particular:

Example 3.2.4.5
.

The categories of
k
-spaces,
hk
-spaces and QCB spaces are closed symmetric monoidal categories under the cartesian product
\times
as the tensor product and the space
C(-,-)
of continuous maps as the internal hom.

The most basic non-cartesian example is the following.

Example 3.2.4.6
.

The category of
R
-modules over a commutative ring
R
is a closed symmetric monoidal category with respect to the tensor product of
R
-modules and the usual internal hom.

More generally, commutative monads give rise to closed symmetric monoidal categories
[
Sea12
, Corollary 2.5.7]
:

Theorem 3.2.4.7
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with all equalisers and let
T
be a commutative monad on
\mathsf{C}
such that the Eilenberg-Moore category
\mathsf{C}^{T}
has all coequalisers. Then
\mathsf{C}^{T}
becomes a closed symmetric monoidal category under the tensor product of
T
-modules.

Most importantly to our cause, this implies that linear
hk
-spaces form a closed symmetric monoidal category:

Example 3.2.4.8
.

The category
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of linear
hk
-spaces is equivalently the category of
F_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules (with
F_{\mathbb{K}}
the free-linear-
hk
-space monad) which satisfies the hypotheses of
3.2.4.7
(since
hk\mathsf{Top}
is a complete category and
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is cocomplete). Hence,
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
is a closed symmetric monoidal category under the tensor product
\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
of
F_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules. The internal hom that arises this way agrees with the space
L(-,-)
from
2.2.2.2
, as can be seen as follows. Let
V,W,Z
be linear
hk
-spaces. By cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
, the set of continuous linear maps
V\to L(W,Z)
is in natural bijection with the set of continuous bilinear maps
V\times W\to Z
. The latter can in turn be identified with the set of continuous linear maps
V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W\to Z
. Hence,
-\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W
is left adjoint to
L(W,-)
and
L(-,-)
is indeed the internal hom associated to the tensor product
\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
.

The internal tensor-hom adjunction

The tensor-hom adjunction “internalises” to the following statement.

Proposition 3.2.4.9

(Internal tensor-hom adjunction)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category. Then we have a natural isomorphism

[A\otimes B,C]\cong[A,[B,C]].\;\;\;\;(A,B,C\in\mathsf{C})

Proof.

Let
D
be any object of
\mathsf{C}
. Applying the (“external”) tensor-hom adjunction several times, we obtain the following chain of natural isomorphisms.

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(D,[A,[B,C]])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(D,[A,[B,C]])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(D\otimes A,[B,C])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(D\otimes A\otimes B,C)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(D,[A\otimes B,C]).

The claim now follows from the Yoneda lemma.
∎

The hom-hom adjunction

Another adjunction arising from the tensor-hom adjunction, crucial for our later considerations on duality theory, is the following.

Proposition 3.2.4.10

(Hom-hom adjunction)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A,[B,C])\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}}([A,C],B),\;\;\;\;(A,B,C\in\mathsf{C})

giving an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-,C]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[-,C]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

Using the tensor-hom adjunction and symmetry of the tensor product,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A,[B,C])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A\otimes B,C)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(B\otimes A,C)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(B,[A,C])

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}}([A,C],B).

∎

As an important corollary, we obtain:

Corollary 3.2.4.11
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category. Then the tensor product
\otimes
preserves all colimits in both variables, and the internal hom functor [-,-] preserves limits in the second variable and sends colimits to limits in the first variable:

\displaystyle(\colim_{i}A_{i})\otimes(\colim_{j}B_{j})

\displaystyle\cong\colim_{i,j}(A_{i}\otimes B_{j}),

\displaystyle[\colim_{i}A_{i},B]

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{i}[A_{i},B],

\displaystyle[A,\lim_{i}B_{i}]

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{i}[A,B_{i}].

Proof.

Let
A
be any object. Then the functors
A\otimes-
,
-\otimes A
are left adjoints and
[A,-]
is a right adjoint (by the tensor-hom adjunction). Furthermore, by the hom-hom adjunction,
3.2.4.10
,

[-,A]:\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathsf{C}

is a left adjoint. The claim now follows from the fact that left adjoints preserve colimits and right adjoints preserve limits.
∎

Day’s reflection theorem

Reflective subcategories of closed symmetric monoidal categories inherit their closed monoidal structure under a number of equivalent simple conditions which are summarised by the following theorem
[
Day72
, Theorem 1.2]
:

Theorem 3.2.4.12
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and suppose that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

is a reflective subcategory with reflector
L
(i.e. left adjoint to the inclusion). Let

\epsilon:A\to LA

be the unit of the adjunction
(\hookrightarrow)\vdash L
and consider the following natural transformations.

\displaystyle\epsilon^{[A,B]}

\displaystyle:[A,B]\to L[A,B],

\displaystyle(A\in\mathsf{C},\,B\in\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle[\epsilon^{A},B]

\displaystyle:[LA,B]\to[A,B],

\displaystyle(A\in\mathsf{C},\,B\in\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle L(\epsilon\otimes B)

\displaystyle:L(A\otimes B)\to L(LA\otimes B),

\displaystyle(A,B\in\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle L(\epsilon\otimes\epsilon)

\displaystyle:L(A\otimes B)\to L(LA\otimes LB).

\displaystyle(A,B\in\mathsf{D})

Then, if one of them is an isomorphism, so are the others. In this case,
\mathsf{D}
becomes a closed symmetric monoidal category with tensor product
L(-\otimes-)
and internal hom
[-,-]
, and
L
is (or rather, admits the structure of) a strong symmetric monoidal functor, thus making the adjunction
(\hookrightarrow)\vdash L
a symmetric monoidal adjunction.

3.3.
Duality: Separated Objects,
*
-Autonomous Categories and the Chu construction

We now discuss a general theory of duality in the setting of closed symmetric monoidal categories which we will subsequently apply to linear
hk
-spaces.

Evaluation morphisms

First, we need a definition of a general version of evaluation mappings and the canonical morphism
\eta:V\to V^{\wedge\wedge}
to the double dual.

Definition 3.3.0.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and let
A,B,D
be objects of
\mathsf{C}
.

1.

The
evaluation morphism
,

e^{A,B}:[A,B]\otimes A\to B,

is given by

e^{A,B}:=(\tau^{[A,B],A,B})^{-1}(\mathsf{id}_{[A,B]}).

2.

The
canonical morphism to the double dual of
A
with respect to
D
is the morphism

\eta^{A,D}:A\to[[A,D],D]

defined as

\eta^{A,D}:=\tau^{A,[A,D],D}(e^{A,D}\circ\sigma^{A,[A,D]}),

where
\sigma
is the braiding (see
3.2.2.1
).

Examining the proof of the hom-hom adjunction (
3.2.4.10
), we see that
\eta^{-,D}
is precisely the unit of this adjunction. In particular,

\eta^{-,D}:\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{C}}\to[[-,D],D]

is a natural transformation.

3.3.1.
Regular monomorphisms, regular images and (co)regular categories

At this point, we would like to ask whether
\eta^{A,D}
is – in a suitable sense – an embedding, and what its image is. This would then yield a general formulation of what it means for the dual
[A,D]
to “separate the points” of
A
and enable the study of
A
in terms of its dual
[A,D]
. The category-theoretic notion of embedding which we would like to adhere to is that of
regular monomorphism
with the corresponding notion of image being the
regular image
. Categories in which these notions are particularly well-behaved are known as
coregular categories
(the dual notion of which is that of a
regular category
).

Regular mono- and epimorphisms

We begin with the notions of regular mono- and epimorphism.

Definition 3.3.1.1
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category. A
regular monomorphism
is a morphism which is the equaliser of some pair of morphisms. A
regular epimorphism
in
\mathsf{C}
is a regular monomorphism in the opposite category
\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}
, i.e. a morphism (in
\mathsf{C}
) which is the
co
equaliser of some pair of morphisms.

As the terminology suggests:

Proposition 3.3.1.2
.

A regular monomorphism is always a monomorphism.

Proof.

By definition, a regular monomorphism
m:A\to B
is the equaliser of a pair of maps
k,l:B\to C
. For any two morphisms
f,g:D\to A
with
m\circ f=m\circ g
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\circ f}

Hence, by the universal property of the equaliser, there is a
unique
morphism
u
making the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Therefore,
f=u=g
. Since
f,g
were arbitrary morphisms with
m\circ f=m\circ g
, this shows that
m
is a monomorphism.
∎

Recall that a
split epimorphism
is a morphism which admits a section (i.e. a right inverse). Dually, a
split monomorphism
is a morphism which admits a retraction (i.e. a left inverse).

Lemma 3.3.1.3
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category with equalisers. Then every split epimorphism (split monomorphism, resp.) is a regular epimorphism (monomorphism, resp.).

Proof.

If
i:A\to B
is is a split monomorphism and
p
is a left inverse of
i
, then
i
is the equaliser of
i\circ p
and
\mathsf{id}_{B}
and hence a regular monomorphism. The case of split epimorphisms is dual.
∎

Characterising (regular, split) mono- and epimorphisms in
hk\mathsf{Top}

In order to be able to apply this general discussion to the examples that we are interested in, we also need to characterise the regular mono- and epimorphisms in the category of
hk
-spaces, where they correspond to important notions of general topology. The following can be found in
[
Str09
, Theorem 3.1]
:

Theorem 3.3.1.4

(Morphisms in
hk\mathsf{Top}
)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous map of
hk
-spaces. Then we have the following equivalences:

\displaystyle f\text{ is a monomorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;f\text{ is injective, }

\displaystyle f\text{ is a regular monomorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;f\text{ is a closed embedding, }

\displaystyle f\text{ is a split monomorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;\text{$X$ is a retract of $Y$ and $f:X\hookrightarrow Y$ is the inclusion, }

and dually,

\displaystyle f\text{ is an epimorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;f\text{ has dense image, }

\displaystyle f\text{ is a regular epimorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;f\text{ is a quotient map, }

\displaystyle f\text{ is a split epimorphism }

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\;\text{ $Y$ is a retract of $X$ and $f:X\twoheadrightarrow Y$ is the retraction. }

Moreover, we have the following permanence properties, where all limits and colimits are taken in
hk\mathsf{Top}
:

1.

Arbitrary products, coproducts, pushouts or composites of regular monomorphisms (i.e. closed embedding, by the above) are regular monomorphisms.

2.

Arbitrary coproducts,
finite
products, pullbacks and composites of regular epimorphisms (i.e quotient maps, by the above) are regular epimorphisms.

Using this characterisation, we may similarly characterise the regular monomorphisms in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
, which we will need in order to apply our general duality theoretic results to the case of linear
hk
-spaces.

Corollary 3.3.1.5
.

Let
V,W
be linear
hk
-spaces and let
i:V\to W
be a continuous linear map. Then
i
is a regular monomorphism if, and only if, it is a regular monomorphism in
hk\mathsf{Top}
, i.e. a closed embedding.

Proof.

For the “if” direction, note that if
i
is a closed embedding, then it is the equaliser of the projection

p:W\to W/i(V)

and the zero map
0:W\to W/i(V)
.

For the other direction, recall that the forgetful functor
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}\to hk\mathsf{Top}
is a right adjoint (to the free linear
hk
-space functor
F_{\mathbb{K}}
) and that right adjoints preserve limits. Hence, if
i
is a regular monomorphism in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
, it is also a regular monomorphism in
hk\mathsf{Top}
.
∎

The regular image

Definition 3.3.1.6
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category and let
f:A\to B
be a morphism.

1.

The
regular image

\im(f)
of
f
is, if it exists, the equaliser of the cokernel pair of
f
, i.e. the equaliser of the pair of morphisms
B\to B\sqcup_{f}B
arising from the pushout diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\sqcup_{f}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

2.

Dually, the
regular coimage

\coim(f)
of
f
is, if it exists, the coequaliser of the kernel pair of
f
. Put differently, the regular coimage of
f
is the image of
f
considered as an morphism in the opposite category
\mathsf{C}
.

3.

The universal property of the pushout yields a unique morphism,

f|^{\im(f)}:A\to\im(f),

the
corestriction of
f
to its image
, making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\im(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\sqcup_{f}B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|^{\im(f)}}

4.

The factorisation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\im(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|^{\im(f)}}

is called the
regular image factorisation
of
f
.

Regular and coregular categories

Definition 3.3.1.7

((Co)regular category,
[
Gra21
]
)
.

A category
\mathsf{C}
is
coregular
if

1.

it has all finite colimits,

2.

the regular image of every morphism in
\mathsf{C}
exists,

3.

and regular monomorphisms are stable under pushouts.

\mathsf{C}
is
regular
if
\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}
is coregular, and
biregular
if it is both regular and coregular.

The following shows that in coregular categories, the regular image behaves as one would hope
[
Gra21
, Theorem 1.11]
:

Theorem 3.3.1.8
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular category let
f
be a morphism. Then:

1.

The corestriction of
f
to its image

f|^{\im(f)}:A\to\im(f)

is an epimorphism. Hence, the regular image factorisation factors
f
into an epimorphism followed by a regular monomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\im(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|^{\im(f)}}

2.

Such factorisation is essentially unique in that for any further epimorphism
e
and regular monomorphism
m
, there is a unique isomorphism
i:I\to\im(f)
making the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\im(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f|^{\im(f)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

In fact, coregular categories are
precisely
those categories in which every morphism can be factored into an epimorphism followed by a regular monomorphism in a well-behaved manner (see
[
Gra21
, Theorem 1.14]
):

Proposition 3.3.1.9
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category with finite colimits in which regular monomorphisms are stable under pullback. Then
\mathsf{C}
is coregular if, and only if, every morphism admits a factorisation into an epimorphism followed by regular monomorphism.

The category of
hk
-spaces is biregular

Cartesian closure is not the only convenience that
hk
-spaces provide. As shown in
[
Str09
, Theorem 3.1 (f)]
:

Proposition 3.3.1.10
.

The category
hk\mathsf{Top}
of
hk
-spaces is biregular, i.e. both a coregular category and a regular category.

Properties of regular monomorphisms in coregular categories

As a final note on (co)regular categories, let us record the following fact (see
[
Gra21
, Proposition 1.13, Lemma 1.3]
for a proof).

Proposition 3.3.1.11
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular category and let
f,g
be morphisms.

1.

The composite
f\circ g
of any pair of regular monomorphisms
f
,
g
is a regular monomorphisms, as well.

2.

If
g\circ f
is a regular monomorphism, then
f
is also a regular monomorphism.

3.

If
f
is both a regular monomorphism and an epimorphism, then
f
is an isomorphism.

3.3.2.
The triple dualisation lemma and separated objects

The triple dualisation lemma

The following simple observation will be crucial.

Lemma 3.3.2.1

(triple dualisation lemma)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be any object of
\mathsf{C}
. Then

[\eta^{C,D},D]\circ\eta^{[C,D],D}=\mathsf{id}_{[C,D]},

i.e. the canonical morphism to the “triple dual” with respect to
D
