(see
3.3.0.1
),

\eta^{[C,D],D}:[C,D]\to[[[C,D],D],D],

has a right inverse, given by

[\eta^{C,D},D]:[[[C,D],D],D]\to[C,D].

In particular,
\eta^{[C,D],D}
is a split (and hence regular) monomorphism.

Proof.

An adjunction can be characterised by the
triangle identities
(see
[
Bor94
, Theorem 3.1.5]
, or any other category theory textbook). The triangle identities for the hom-hom adjunction (see
3.2.4.10
) reduce to a single identity (because of the “self-adjoint” nature of this adjunction), and this identity is given precisely by:

[\eta^{C,D},D]\circ\eta^{[C,D],D}=\mathsf{id}_{[C,D]}.

∎

Separated objects in a closed symmetric monoidal category

The triple dualisation lemma suggests the following definition.

Definition 3.3.2.2
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. An object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
is
D
-separated
if the canonical map (see
3.3.0.1
).

\eta^{A,D}:A\to[[A,D],D]

is a regular monomorphism.

Example 3.3.2.3
.

Let
K
be a field. Then every (algebraic)
K
-vector space is
K
-separated (assuming the axiom of choice).

The example that will be most important to our context is the following.

Example 3.3.2.4
.

The
\mathbb{K}
-separated objects in the closed symmetric monoidal category
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of linear
hk
-spaces are precisely the
replete
linear
hk
-spaces. Recall that a replete linear
hk
-space is a linear
hk
-space for which the canonical map
V\to V^{\wedge\wedge}
is a closed embedding (
1.4.2.1
). Since regular monomorphisms in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
are exactly given by closed embeddings (
3.3.1.5
), this is exactly the definition of a
\mathbb{K}
-separated object in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
.

Remark 3.3.2.5
.

The notion of
D
-separated object can be seen as a special case of a construction related to the
(enriched) idempotent core of a monad
, see
[
LW14
, Example 4.18]
.

Some permanence properties of separated objects

It turns out that the category of
D
-separated objects enjoys excellent permanence properties, thanks to the triple dualisation lemma. The next proposition is a first step towards establishing this.

Proposition 3.3.2.6
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. Then:

1.

For any object
A
,
[A,D]
is
D
-separated.

2.

If
B
is
D
-separated and
\iota:A\hookrightarrow B
is a regular monomorphism, then
A
is
D
-separated, as well. In particular, an object
A
is
D
-separated if, and only if, it admits a regular monomorphism into
some
dual
[B,D]
with respect to
D
.

3.

For all objects
A,B\in\mathsf{C}
, if
B
is
D
-separated, then
[A,B]
is also
D
-separated.

Proof.

1.

This follows directly from the triple dualisation lemma (
3.3.2.1
).

2.

By naturality of
\eta
, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[[B,D],D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[[A,D],D]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{B,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{A,D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[[\iota,D],D]}

By
3.3.1.11
, the composition of regular monomorphisms is a regular monomorphism and hence,

h:=\eta^{B,D}\circ\iota

is a regular monomorphism. Since
h=[[\iota,D],D]\circ\eta^{A,D}
(by commutativity of the above diagram),
\eta^{A,D}
is a regular monomorphism as well, by
3.3.1.11
. This shows that
A
is
D
-separated.

3.

Since
B
is
D
-separated,
\eta^{B,D}
is a regular monomorphism. Since the internal hom preserves limits in the second variable,

[A,\eta^{B,D}]:[A,B]\to[A,[[B,D],D]]

is also a regular monomorphism. Hence,
[A,B]
admits a regular monomorphism into a
D
-separated object and is therefore
D
-separated, by the previous point.

∎

The separation functor

As we will see,
D
-separated objects form a reflective subcategory of the given category (if the latter is sufficiently well-behaved). Let us first define the would-be reflector, the
D
-separation
.

Definition 3.3.2.7

(
D
-Separation)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. We define the
D
-separation functor
as the regular image (see
3.3.1.6
),

\mathsf{sep}_{D}:=\im(\eta^{-,D}),

of the natural transformation
\eta^{-,D}
in the functor category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathsf{C}}
.

Since limits and colimits in functor categories are computed pointwise (see, for example,
[
Rie17
, Proposition 3.3.9]
), the
D
-separation
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)
of an
object

A
is the regular image in
\mathsf{C}
of the canonical morphism to the double dual of
A
with respect to
D
. The regular image factorisation of
\eta^{-,D}
furthermore yields two natural transformations,

A\to\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)\hookrightarrow[[A,D],D],\;\;\;\;(A\in\mathsf{C}

the corestriction of
\eta^{A,D}
to its regular image, and the inclusion of
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)
into
[[A,D],D]
.

Of course, the term “
D
-separation” is only justified if the
D
-separation of an object is
D
-separated. This is indeed the case.

Proposition 3.3.2.8
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
A,D
be objects of
\mathsf{C}
. Then:

1.

\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)
is always
D
-separated.

2.

The following are equivalent:

2.1.

A
is
D
-separated.

2.2.

The co-restriction of
\eta^{A,D}
to its regular image,

\eta^{A,D}|^{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)}:A\to\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A),

is an isomorphism.

2.3.

There is
some
isomorphism

A\cong\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A).

In particular, we can use the second point to reformulate the first as saying:

\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A))\cong\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A).

Proof.

1.

\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)
admits a regular monomorphism into a dual with respect to
D
, namely the inclusion

\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)\hookrightarrow[[A,D],D].

Hence, the claim follows from
3.3.2.6
.

2.

If
A
is
D
-separated, i.e.
\eta^{A,D}
is a regular monomorphism, then
\eta^{A,D}|^{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)}
is a regular monomorphism, as well (by
3.3.1.11
). Since
\eta^{A,D}|^{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)}
is also an epimorphism, it is hence an isomorphism (by
3.3.1.11
).
On the other hand, if
\eta^{A,D}|^{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)}
is an isomorphism, then
\eta^{A,D}
is a regular monomorphism, so
A
is
D
-separated.

The remaining implications follow from the fact that
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A)
is always D-separated (this was the first point) and that the property of being
D
-separated is isomorphism-invariant.

∎

This also shows that the essential image of the
D
-separation functor
\mathsf{sep}_{D}
is given precisely be the
D
-separated objects. The full subcategory of a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{C}
spanned by the
D
-separated objects is therefore appropriately denoted by

\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}.

Definition 3.3.2.9
.

For the case of replete linear
hk
-spaces and
D=\mathbb{K}
(see
Example

3.3.2.4
), we write

r:=\mathsf{sep}_{\mathbb{K}}

for the
\mathbb{K}
-separation functor and call this functor the
repletion
. Accordingly, the category of replete linear
hk
-spaces will be denoted by

r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}:=\mathsf{sep}_{\mathbb{K}}{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}.

Unfolding definitions, the repletion
rV
of a linear
hk
-space is precisely the closure of its image in the double dual
V^{\wedge\wedge}
under the canonical map
V\to V^{\wedge\wedge}
.

D
-separated objects form a reflective subcategory

By now, the reader might expect, as the name would suggest, that the
D
-separation is the reflector associated to the
reflective
subcategory of
D
-separated objects. That this expectation is indeed met is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3.2.10
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. Then the
D
-separation functor (co-restricted to its image),

\mathsf{sep}_{D}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{C},

is left adjoint to the inclusion

\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}.

Proof.

In order to simplify notation, write

(-,-):=\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(-,-),

\eta^{A}:=\eta^{A,D},

and,

\mathsf{sep}:=\mathsf{sep}_{D},\;\;j^{A}:=\eta^{A}|^{\mathsf{sep}A}.

In order to obtain an adjunction,

(\mathsf{sep}A,B)\cong(A,B),\;\;\;\;\;\;(A,B\in\mathsf{C})

define two natural transformations
\Phi
,
\Psi
with components

\Phi^{A,B}:(\mathsf{sep}A,B)\to(A,B),\;\;\Phi^{A,B}:=(j^{A},B)

\Psi^{A,B}:(A,B)\to(\mathsf{sep}A,B),\;\;\Psi^{A,B}:=(\mathsf{sep}(A),(j^{B})^{-1})\circ\mathsf{sep}^{A,B},

where

\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}:(A,B)\to(\mathsf{sep}(A),\mathsf{sep}(B)),\;\;f\mapsto\mathsf{sep}(f)

is the action of the
D
-separation functor on morphisms
A\to B
.
Fix two objects
A,B
and write
\Phi:=\Phi^{A,B}
,
\Psi:=\Psi^{A,B}
. We claim that
\Phi
and
\Psi
are inverse to one another, which would complete the proof. We will show this in two steps.

1.

First, we claim that

j^{\mathsf{sep}(A)}=\mathsf{sep}(j^{A}).

This can be seen as follows. By naturality of
j
, the diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sep}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sep}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sep}(\mathsf{sep}(A))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sep}(j^{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\mathsf{sep}(A)}}

commutes, i.e.

j^{\mathsf{sep}(A)}\circ j^{A}=\mathsf{sep}(j^{A})\circ j^{A}.

Since
j^{A}
is an epimorphism (by
3.3.1.8
), this implies the (intermediate) claim.

2.

Now, using naturality of
j
, we find that

\displaystyle\Phi\circ\Psi(f)

\displaystyle=(j^{A},B)\circ(\mathsf{sep}(A),(j^{B})^{-1})\circ\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}(f)

\displaystyle=(j^{A},B)((\mathsf{sep}(A),(j^{B})^{-1})(\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}(f))

\displaystyle=(j^{A},B)((j^{B})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}(f))

\displaystyle=(j^{B})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}(f)\circ j^{A}

\displaystyle=f,

for all
f\in(A,B)
. Likewise, for every
g\in(\mathsf{sep}A,B)
,

\displaystyle\Psi\circ\Phi

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{sep}(A),(j^{B})^{-1})\circ\mathsf{sep}^{A,B}\circ(j^{A},B)(g)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{sep}(A),(j^{B})^{-1})(\mathsf{sep}((j^{A},B)(g)))

\displaystyle=(j^{B})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{sep}(g\circ j^{A}))

\displaystyle=(j^{B})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{sep}(g)\circ\mathsf{sep}(j^{A})

(by functoriality of
\mathsf{sep}
)

\displaystyle=(j^{B})^{-1}\circ\mathsf{sep}(g)\circ j^{\mathsf{sep}(A)}

(by the previous step)

\displaystyle=g.

(by naturality of
j
)

This completes the proof.
∎

The adjunction from
3.3.2.10
can be enhanced to an internal (or
enriched
) version.

Corollary 3.3.2.11
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. Then we have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle[A,B]\cong[\mathsf{sep}_{D}(A),B].\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(A\in\mathsf{C},B\in\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{C})

Proof.

Let
C
be any object. By
3.3.2.6
, for any
D
-separated object
B
,
[C,B]
is
D
-separated, as well. Combining this fact with
3.3.2.10
, we get the following chain of natural isomorphisms.

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(C,[\mathsf{sep}(A),B])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{sep}(A),[C,B])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(A,[C,B])

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(C,[A,B]).

The claim now follows from the Yoneda lemma.
∎

D
-separated objects form a closed symmetric monoidal category

Day’s reflection theorem (
3.2.4.12
) now immediately yields:

Corollary 3.3.2.12
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be any object. Then the category
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
of
D
-separated objects is a closed symmetric monoidal category with tensor product
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(-\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}-)
and internal hom
[-,-]_{\mathsf{C}}
, and the
D
-separation functor is a strong symmetric monoidal functor

\mathsf{sep}_{D}:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C}).

Applying this Corollary to the case of replete linear
hk
-spaces, we obtain:

Corollary 3.3.2.13
.

The category
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of replete linear
hk
-spaces becomes a closed symmetric monoidal category under the internal hom
L(-,-)
and tensor product

V\,\otimes_{r}W:=r(V\otimes W),\qquad(V,W\in r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})})

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces (equivalently,
F_{\mathbb{K}}
-modules, see
Example

3.2.4.8
) and
r=\mathsf{sep}_{\mathbb{K}}
is the repletion functor (
3.3.2.9
), i.e. the
\mathbb{K}
-separation functor in the category of linear
hk
-spaces.

3.3.3.
*
-Autonomous categories

Definition 3.3.3.1
.

A
*
-autonomous category
is a closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{C}
together with an object
D
, the
dualising object
, such that

\eta^{A,D}:A\to[[A,D],D],

is an isomorphism for all
A\in\mathsf{C}
.
In this case, we denote by
(-)^{*}:=[-,D]
the “dualisation functor”,

(-)^{*}:\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}\to\mathsf{C},

with respect to
D
, which is then an equivalence of categories.

Hence,
*
-autonomous categories are “self-dual”,

\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}\cong\mathsf{C},

a property with far-reaching consequences. For example, a
*
-autonomous category
\mathsf{C}
which has all limits automatically admits all colimits as well (and vice versa). After all, colimits in
\mathsf{C}
are just limits in the opposite category
\mathsf{C}^{\text{op}}
(which is equivalent to
\mathsf{C}
by
*
-autonomy). Of course, self-duality entails more generally that for
any
category-theoretic property of
\mathsf{C}
(in the sense of being invariant under equivalence of categories), its dual property is also true of
\mathsf{C}
.

Example 3.3.3.2
.

The category of finite-dimensional vector spaces over a field
K
is a
*
-autonomous category with respect to the standard tensor product
\otimes
and internal hom, with
K
as the dualising object.

3.3.4.
The Chu construction

A general method to construct
*
-autonomous categories from closed symmetric monoidal categories is given by the
Chu construction
(given originally in
[
Chu78
]
).

Definition 3.3.4.1

(Chu construction,
[
Bar91
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. Then the category

\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})

is defined as follows.

1.

An object of
\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is a triple
(A,A^{\top},a)
, where

1.1.

A,A^{\top}
are objects of
\mathsf{C}
, and

1.2.

a:A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A^{\top}\to D
is a morphism in
\mathsf{C}
.

2.

A morphism
(A,A^{\top},a)\to(B,B^{\top},b)
in
\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is a pair
(f,f^{\top})
, where

2.1.

f:A\to B
,
f^{\top}:B^{\top}\to A^{\top}
are morphisms in
\mathsf{C}
,

2.2.

making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes B^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes f^{\top}}

To understand this definition, it is helpful to consider the case when
\mathsf{C}
is the category of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces and the dualising object is
\mathbb{K}
. In this case, an object of
\mathsf{Chu}_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathsf{Vect})
is a pair of vector spaces equipped with a bilinear pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
to the base field
\mathbb{K}
, and a morphism,

(V,V^{\top},\langle-,-\rangle_{V})\to(W,W^{\top},\langle-,-\rangle_{W}),

is a pair of linear maps
(f,f^{\top})
that are “adjoint” to one another,

\langle f(v),w^{\top}\rangle_{W}=\langle v,f(w^{\top})\rangle_{V}.\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(v\in V,w^{\top}\in W^{\top})

Let us now describe the dualising object, internal hom and tensor product on
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
.

Definition 3.3.4.2
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a closed symmetric monoidal category with pullbacks and let
\mathcal{A}=(A,A^{\top},a)
,
\mathcal{B}=(B,B^{\top},b)
be two objects of
\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
.

1.

Define dualising object of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
as

\mathcal{D}:=(D,I_{\mathsf{C}},\rho),

where
I_{\mathsf{C}}
is the tensor unit and

\rho:D\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}I_{\mathsf{C}}\to D

is the right unitor.

2.

The internal hom

[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}

is given by the triple
([A,B]_{0},A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top},c)
, where:

2.1.

[A,B]_{0}
is given by the pullback,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B^{\top},A^{\top}]_{\mathsf{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]_{\mathsf{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A\otimes B^{\top},D]_{\mathsf{C}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Here,
\phi
is obtained via the tensor-hom adjunction from the composite,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}[B^{\top},A^{\top}]_{\mathsf{C}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{A}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{eval}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

and, similarly,
\psi
is obtained from the composite,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[A,B]_{\mathsf{C}}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{eval}\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\mathsf{id}_{B^{\top}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

2.2.

The definition of
[A,B]_{0}
yields a map

[A,B]_{0}\to[A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top},D],

which induces the pairing

c:[A,B]_{0}\otimes(A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}B^{\top})\to D

via the tensor-hom adjunction.

3.

Writing
(-)^{*}:=[-,\mathcal{D}]_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}
, the tensor product of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is defined as

\mathcal{A}\otimes_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B}:=([\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}^{*}]_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})})^{*}.

With this structure in place, we obtain
[
Bar91
, p. 8, Theorem 4.3]
:

Theorem 3.3.4.3
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. Then
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is a bicomplete
*
-autonomous category. Moreover, the functor

\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C}),\;\;A\mapsto(A,[A,D],e^{A,D}\circ\sigma^{A,[A,D]}),\;f\mapsto(f,[f,D])

is fully faithful, strongly symmetric monoidal, as well as left adjoint and right inverse to the functor,

{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{C},\;\;(A,A^{\top},a)\mapsto A,\;(f,f^{\top})\mapsto f,

The extensional-separated Chu construction

Denoting by
\mathsf{Vect}
the category of vector spaces over a field
K
, the objects of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{K}(\mathsf{Vect})
are
almost
what is known in the context of functional analysis as a
dual system
(or
paired vector space
, see
1.4.4.1
). The only difference is that for
(V,V^{\top},\langle-,-\rangle)
to be a dual system, one requires the pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
to be a
perfect pairing
. This means that both

V\to(V^{\top})^{\prime},\;\;\;v\mapsto\langle v,-\rangle

and

V^{\top}\to V^{\prime},\;\;\;v^{\top}\mapsto\langle-,v^{\top}\rangle

are to be injective. The injectivity of the first of these maps expresses that
V^{\top}

separates the points
of
V
. The injectivity of the second map, on the other hand, enables us to view elements of
V^{\top}
as genuine functionals on
V
. Put differently, it expresses
function extensionality
for elements of
V^{\top}
.

These separation and extensionality requirements can be extended to the general case of the Chu construction to yield a further way of constructing
*
-autonomous categories.

Definition 3.3.4.4

(Extensional/separated objects)
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular, closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. An object
(A,A^{\top},a)
of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is called

1.

extensional
if the morphism,

\phi:A^{\top}\to[A,D]_{\mathsf{C}},

induced from
a
(via the tensor-hom adjunction), is a regular monomorphism, and

2.

separated
if the morphism,

\psi:A\to[A^{\top},D]_{\mathsf{C}},

induced from
a
, is a regular monomorphism.

Define the
separation
(or
pair separation
in distinction to the
D
-separation of
3.3.2.7
) of
(A,A^{\top},a)
as the triple
(\im(\psi),A^{\top},\tilde{a})
, where

1.

\im(\psi)
is the regular image of
\psi
, and

2.

\tilde{a}
is given by the composite,

\im(\psi)\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A^{\top}\to[A^{\top},D]\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}A^{\top}\to D.

Similarly, define the
extensionalisation
as the triple
(A,\im(\phi),\overline{a})
, where

1.

\im(\phi)
is the regular image of
\phi
, and

2.

\overline{a}
is the composite,

A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}\im(\phi)\to A\otimes_{\mathsf{C}}[A,D]\to D.

Finally, denote full subcategories of extensional (separated, resp.) objects of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
by
e\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
(
s\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
, resp.), and write

\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})

for the full subcategory of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
spanned by those objects which are both separated
and
extensional.

Remark 3.3.4.5
.

In
[
Bar98
]
, Barr defines the separated-extensional Chu category
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
with respect to arbitrary factorisation systems. Coregularity of the category
\mathsf{C}
ensures the existence of unique regular image factorisations, so that our definition is that of Barr for the special case of the regular image factorisation system. Moreover, this factorisation system also has the following two properties that Barr requires of the given factorisation system
\mathcal{E}/\mathcal{M}
.

1.

Every morphism in
\mathcal{E}
is an epimorphism.

2.

For every object
A
, if
m\in\mathcal{M}
, then
[A,m]_{\mathsf{C}}\in\mathcal{M}
.

For the regular image factorisation system in a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category, the first condition holds by definition of this factorisation system, while the other one follows from then fact that the internal hom preserves limits in the second variable.

Example 3.3.4.6
.

The objects of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathsf{Vect})
are now exactly dual systems, or “paired vector spaces”, in the sense of functional-analytic duality theory (mentioned before in the beginning of
Section

3.3.4
, see
1.4.4.1
for a definition).

We will need the following facts on the relation between the extensionalisation and (pair) separation functors
e
and
s

[
Bar98
, Proposition 3.2, Proposition 3.4]
:

Proposition 3.3.4.7
.

Lt
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object of
\mathsf{C}
. The extensionalisation
e
and separation
s
extend to functors that are right (left, resp.) adjoint to the inclusions of the full subcategories of extensional (separated, resp.) objects.

Moreover, the tensor unit of
\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is extensional and the full subcategory
e\mathsf{Chu}(\mathsf{C})
of extensional objects is closed under tensor products. Hence,
e\mathsf{Chu}(\mathsf{C})
is a symmetric monoidal category and the inclusion functor from
\mathsf{C}
into
\mathsf{Chu}(\mathsf{C})
co-restricts to a strong symmetric monoidal functor

\mathsf{C}\to e\mathsf{Chu}(\mathsf{C}).

We now describe the closed monoidal structure on
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
.

Definition 3.3.4.8
.

Define

1.

the dualising object and tensor unit of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
as
e\mathcal{D}
and
s\mathcal{I}
, respectively (where
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{I}
are those of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
),

2.

the internal hom of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
as

[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]_{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}:=e[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})},

3.

and the tensor product as

\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B}:=s(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B}).

Theorem 3.3.4.9
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D
be an object. Then, with the structure described in
3.3.4.8
,
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is a bicomplete
*
-autonomous category. Moreover, the functor

\mathsf{C}\to\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C}),\;\;A\mapsto s(A,[A,D],e^{A,D}\circ\sigma^{A,[A,D]})

is a strong symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof.

For
*
-autonomy of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
, see
[
Bar98
]
. For the second part of the statement, notice the above functor is the composite of the functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e\mathsf{Chu}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

the first of which is strong symmetric monoidal by
3.3.4.7
. The second, on the other hand, is a
strict
monoidal functor, by definition of the tensor product on
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
:

\mathcal{A}\otimes_{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B}=s(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B})=s(\mathcal{A}\otimes_{e\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}\mathcal{B})

The claim now follows from the fact that a composite of strong symmetric monoidal functors is strong symmetric monoidal.
∎

One important difference between the categories
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
and
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is the following.

Proposition 3.3.4.10
.

The functor

\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{C},\;\;\;(A,A^{\top},a)\mapsto A

is faithful.

Proof.

If
(f,f_{0}^{\top}),(f,f_{1}^{\top}):(A,A^{\top},a)\to(B,B^{\top},b)
are two morphisms in
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
inducing the same morphism
f
in
\mathsf{C}
under the above functor, then, since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\top}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B,D]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[B,D]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}^{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f,D]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\top}}

commutes and the morphism
A^{\top}\to[A,D]
is a monomorphism,
f_{0}^{\top}=f_{1}^{\top}
.
∎

We may hence interpret the above functor as a forgetful functor. Under this interpretation, the objects of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
are
\mathsf{C}
-objects with some additional structure (which the forgetful functor “forgets”), and the morphisms in
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
are precisely those
\mathsf{C}
-morphisms between (underlying
\mathsf{C}
-objects of) objects of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
which preserve this additional structure.

In particular, if
\mathsf{C}
is a concrete category (i.e. equipped with a faithful functor to the category of sets), then so is
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
in a canonical way. This is in sharp contrast to the larger category
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
. For example, the triple
(0,V,0)
, where
V
is
any
(algebraic) vector space, is an object of
{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{Vect}(\mathsf{Set}))
. Such “degenerate” examples cannot occur in
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{Vect}(\mathsf{Set}))
.

Relation between the separated-extensional Chu construction and
D
-separated objects

A natural question is now which objects of
\mathsf{C}
can arise as the underlying
\mathsf{C}
-object of an an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
. In other words: which
\mathsf{C}
-objects admit the structure of an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
? These are precisely the
D
-separated objects.

Proposition 3.3.4.11
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a coregular bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category and let
D\in\mathsf{C}
. Then an object
A
of
\mathsf{C}
is
D
-separated if, and only if, it is the underlying
\mathsf{C}
-object of an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
(i.e. there is an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
of the form
(A,A^{\top},a)
). As a consequence, the functor

\iota:\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C})\hookrightarrow\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C}),\;\;A\mapsto(A,[A,D],e^{A,D}\circ\sigma^{A,[A,D]}),\;f\mapsto(f,[f,D])

is well-defined, fully faithful and left adjoint as well as right inverse to

\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C}),\;\;\;(A,A^{\top},a)\mapsto A.

In other words,
\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
is a coreflective subcategory of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
and we have the following diagram of functors, in which the solid square of inclusions commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Chu}_{D}(\mathsf{C})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Proof.

If
A\in\mathsf{C}
is
D
-separated, then
(A,[A,D],e^{A,D}\circ\sigma^{A,[A,D]})
is an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
. Conversely, if
(A,A^{\top},a)\in\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{C})
, then we have a regular monomorphism,

A\to[A^{\top},D],

and by
3.3.2.6
,
A
is
D
-separated. The other claims now follow from
3.3.4.3
.
∎

3.3.5.
Closed symmetric monoidal categories over cartesian closed categories

The following notion captures when a given closed symmetric monoidal (or
*
-autonomous, in particular) category stands in a good relationship with a cartesian closed “base category”.

Definition 3.3.5.1
.

A
closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category

|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
is a closed symmetric monoidal category
\mathsf{D}
, together with a cartesian closed category
\mathsf{C}
(the
base category
) and a symmetric monoidal adjunction (
3.2.2.3
),

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

By a bicomplete, coregular closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
we mean a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category for which both
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{C}
are bicomplete (i.e. have all limits and colimits) and coregular. A
*
-autonomous-over-cartesian-closed category
is a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
where
\mathsf{D}
is
*
-autonomous.

We cal call
\mathsf{C}
the base category and the adjunction
|\cdot|\vdash T
the free-forgetful adjunction.

Remark 3.3.5.2
.

A definition similar to
3.3.5.1
is given in
[
dP14
, p. 12, Definition 6]
under the name
linear-non-linear category
, in the context of (the categorical semantics of)
linear logic
.

In our context, one should think of
\mathsf{D}
as a category of linear spaces (e.g. vector spaces or linear
hk
-spaces) living over a base category
\mathsf{C}
of “general” spaces (e.g. sets or
hk
-spaces).

The free-forgetful adjunction defining a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category is always enriched over the base category:

Proposition 3.3.5.3
.

Let
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
be a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category. Then we have a natural isomorphism,

[X,V]_{\mathsf{C}}\cong|[TX,V]_{\mathsf{D}}|.\qquad(X\in\mathsf{C},V\in\mathsf{D})

Proof.

Using that the free-forgetful adjunction is a monoidal adjunction, we obtain a sequence of natural isomorphisms,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(Y,[X,V]_{\mathsf{C}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(Y\times X,V)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(T(Y\times X),V)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(TY\otimes_{\mathsf{D}}TX,V)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(TY,[TX,V]_{\mathsf{D}})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(Y,|[TX,V]_{\mathsf{D}}|).\qquad(Y\in\mathsf{C})

The claim now follows from the Yoneda lemma.
∎

Free
D
-separated objects

The notion of
D
-separated object is compatible with that of closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category, in the following sense.

Proposition 3.3.5.4
.

Let
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
be a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category and let
D\in\mathsf{D}
be an object. Then

T_{D}:=\mathsf{sep}_{D}\circ T

is left adjoint to the “forgetful” functor

|\cdot|\circ\iota:\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C},

where

\iota:\mathsf{sep}_{D}\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{D}

is the inclusion functor, and

|\cdot|\circ\iota\vdash T_{D}:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}

is again a closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category.

Proof.

The
D
-separation
\mathsf{sep}_{D}
is left adjoint to the inclusion functor
\iota
and this is a symmetric monoidal adjunction, since
D
-separated objects form an exponential ideal in
\mathsf{D}
. Composing the two symmetric monoidal adjunctions
\iota\vdash\mathsf{sep}_{D}
and
|\cdot|\vdash T
gives the desired

|\cdot|\circ\iota\vdash T_{D}.

∎

The free replete linear
hk
-space

As a particular case, the category
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of replete linear
hk
-spaces also admits
free objects
.

Definition 3.3.5.5

(Free replete linear
hk
-space)
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Define the
free linear
hk
-space
on
X
,

\mathcal{M}_{c}(X):=rF_{\mathbb{K}}(X),

as the repletion of the free linear
hk
-space on
X
.

Remark 3.3.5.6
.

The notation
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
will be justified in Chapter 4, where we will show that this object can be identified with the space of compactly supported Radon measures on
X
, when
X
is a Hausdorff
k
-space.

Warning 3.3.5.7
.

We will use the same notation for the free
paired
linear
hk
-space (and have already done so in the introduction). This unproblematic, since these two objects agree, in the sense that the free replete linear
hk
-space
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
carries a natural paired linear
hk
-space structure under which it becomes the free paired linear
hk
-space.

Corollary 3.3.5.8

(Universal property of
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
)
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space, let
V
be a replete linear
hk
-space and
f:X\to V
be a continuous map. Then there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{f}:\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)\to V
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute, where
\delta_{\bullet}
is the composite of the canonical maps,

X\to F_{\mathbb{K}}(X)\to rF_{\mathbb{K}}(X).

A construction theorem for
*
-Autonomous-over-cartesian-closed categories

The following is a general construction theorem which uses the separated-extensional Chu construction to obtain a
*
-autonomous-over-cartesian-closed category
U\vdash M_{D}:\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D})\to\mathsf{C}
from a general closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
in such a way that if
|\cdot|:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
is a faithful functor, then so is
U:\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D})\to\mathsf{C}
.

Theorem 3.3.5.9
.

Let
|\cdot|\vdash T:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
be a bicomplete, coregular closed-symmetric-monoidal-over-cartesian-closed category
\mathsf{C}
, and let
D\in\mathsf{D}
be an object. Then the forgetful functor,

U:\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D})\to\mathsf{C},\;\;\;(A,A^{\top},\langle-,-\rangle)\mapsto|A|,

has a strong symmetric monoidal left adjoint
M_{D}
. Hence, the resulting adjunction,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{D}}

defines a
*
-autonomous-over-cartesian-closed category.

Proof.

By
3.3.2.10
and
3.3.4.11
, we have the following adjunctions,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{sep}_{D}(\mathsf{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{sep}_{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

Hence,

M_{D}:=\iota\circ\mathsf{sep}_{D}\circ T

is the desired left adjoint. It remains to show that
M_{D}
is (or rather, admits the structure of a) strong symmetric monoidal functor. To see this, notice that
M_{D}
also factors as the composite of two strong symmetric monoidal functors,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{D}(\mathsf{D}),}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}

where the functor on the right is the functor as described in
3.3.4.9
.
∎

Paired linear
hk
-spaces

Applying the extensional-separated Chu construction to the category of linear
hk
-spaces, we obtain
paired linear
hk
-spaces
.

Definition 3.3.5.10
.

A
paired linear
hk
-space
is an object of
\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{\mathbb{K}}({\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})})
, i.e. a linear
hk
-space
V
together with a further linear
hk
-space
V^{*}
and a bilinear map

\langle-,-\rangle:V\times V^{*}\to\mathbb{K}

such that both

V\to(V^{*})^{\wedge},\;\;x\mapsto\langle x,-\rangle

and

V^{*}\to V^{\wedge},\;\;\phi\mapsto\langle-,\phi\rangle

are regular monomorphisms in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
.

Since, by
3.3.1.5
, regular monomorphisms in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
are equivalently closed embeddings, we can reformulate this definition as follows, identifying
V^{*}
with its image in
V^{\wedge}
.

Definition 3.3.5.11

(Paired linear
hk
-space, more explicitly)
.

A
paired linear
hk
-space
is a linear
hk
-space
V
together with a closed linear subspace
V^{*}\subseteq V^{\wedge}
of the natural dual such that

V\to(V^{*})^{\wedge},\;\;x\mapsto\langle x,-\rangle

is a closed embedding. A morphism of paired linear
hk
-spaces
V,W
is a continuous linear map
f:V\to W
such that
f^{\wedge}(W^{*})\subseteq V^{*}
, where

f^{\wedge}:W^{\wedge}\to V^{\wedge},\;\;\phi\mapsto\phi\circ f

is the adjoint of
f
. We denote the category of paired linear
hk
-spaces by

\mathsf{L}:=\widehat{\mathsf{Chu}}_{\mathbb{K}}({\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}).

We denote the internal hom and tensor product of paired linear
hk
-spaces simply by

[V,W]:=[V,W]_{\mathsf{L}},\;\;V\,\widehat{\otimes}\,W:=V\otimes_{\mathsf{L}}W\qquad\qquad(V,W\in\mathsf{L})

as long as there is no potential for ambiguity. The underlying linear
hk
-space of
[V,\mathbb{K}]
is exactly
V^{*}
, so it is justified to also write

V^{*}:=[V,\mathbb{K}]\qquad\qquad(V\in\mathsf{L})

for the dual, viewed as a paired linear
hk
-space.

As a special case of
3.3.5.9
, we immediately obtain that
\mathsf{L}
is a
*
-autonomous category with respect to the above tensor product and internal hom.

Remark 3.3.5.12
.

By construction, the internal hom
[V,W]
of paired linear
hk
-spaces
V,W
is the closed subspace

[V,W]\subseteq L(V,W)

of the space of continuous linear maps
L(V,W)
(the internal hom in
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
and also
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
) consisting of those continuous linear maps which are morphisms of paired linear
hk
-spaces.

The category of replete linear
hk
-spaces embeds into the category of paired linear
hk
-spaces

A wide range of examples of paired linear
hk
-spaces is supplied by considering replete linear
hk
-spaces as paired linear
hk
-spaces, as follows.

Proposition 3.3.5.13
.

The functor

\iota:r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L},\;\;V\mapsto(V,V^{\wedge}),\;f\mapsto f^{\wedge}

is fully faithful, displaying
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathsf{L}
.

Proof.

This is a special case of
3.3.4.11
.
∎

As a consequence, when
W
is any paired linear
hk
-space and
V
is in the essential image of the inclusion functor,

r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L},

i.e.
V^{*}=V^{\wedge}
, then every continuous linear map
V\to W
is a morphism of paired linear
hk
-spaces and we have,

[V,W]=L(V,W).

Fréchet and Brauner spaces are paired linear
k
-spaces in a unique way

While for a general replete linear
hk
-space
V
, there may a priori be several paired linear
hk
-spaces whose underlying linear
hk
-space is
V
, this is not possible for Fréchet or Brauner spaces: their dual is, in some sense, uniquely determined.

Proposition 3.3.5.14
.

Let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space with dual
V^{*}
and suppose that the underlying linear
hk
-space of
V
is a Fréchet space or a Brauner space. Then
V^{*}=V^{\wedge}
.

Proof.

Notice that the assumption that

j:V\to(V^{*})^{\wedge},\;\;\;x\mapsto\langle x,-\rangle

is a closed embedding implies that

j^{\wedge}:(V^{*})^{\wedge\wedge}\to V^{\wedge},\;\;\;\chi\mapsto(x\mapsto\chi(\langle x,-\rangle))

is surjective, using the Hahn-Banach extension theorem (which is applicable because both
V
and
(V^{*})^{\wedge}
are Fréchet/Brauner spaces). By Smith duality, the canonical map

\eta^{V^{*}}:V^{*}\to(V^{*})^{\wedge\wedge}

is an isomorphism. Hence, observing that

j^{\wedge}\circ\eta^{V^{*}}:V^{*}\to V^{\wedge},\;\;\phi\mapsto\phi,

is the embedding of
V^{*}
into
V^{\wedge}
, we see that this map is surjective and hence the identity.
∎

The free paired linear
hk
-space

By
3.3.5.9
, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{L}\to hk\mathsf{Top}
admits a left adjoint, and this left adjoint is given by the composition of the left adjoint
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
to the forgetful functor
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}\to hk\mathsf{Top}
, followed by the inclusion of
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
into
\mathsf{L}
. Hence, we are justified in defining (see also
3.3.5.7
):

Definition 3.3.5.15
.

We denote the left adjoint to the forgetful functor
\mathsf{L}\to hk\mathsf{Top}
by
\mathcal{M}_{c}
. For an
hk
-space
X
, we call
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
the
free paired linear
hk
-space
on
X
.

Remark 3.3.5.16
.

The notation
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
will be justified in Chapter 4, where we will show that the elements of
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
can be identified with certain compactly supported measures. Moreover, by
3.3.5.3
, the free-forgetful adjunction between paired linear
hk
-spaces and
hk
-spaces is
hk\mathsf{Top}
-enriched,

[\mathcal{M}_{c}(X),V]\cong C(X,V),\qquad(X\in hk\mathsf{Top},V\in\mathsf{L})

and we will see in Chapter 4 that this natural isomorphism is given by vector-valued integration (see
Remark

4.6.1.4
).

Remark 3.3.5.17
.

Since by
3.3.5.9
, the free-forgetful adjunction between paired linear
hk
-spaces and
hk
-spaces is a symmetric monoidal adjunction, it induces a symmetric monoidal, i.e. commutative, monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}
with underlying endofunctor
X\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
.

Chapter 4
Monadic Measure and Integration Theory

In this chapter, we will harvest the fruits of the general abstract developments of Chapter 3. To this end, we will first define the linear
hk
-space
C_{b}(X)
of continuous bounded functions on an
hk
-space
X
. Then, we will construct
\mathcal{M}(X)
as a space of continuous linear functionals on
C_{b}(X)
and endow
\mathcal{M}(X)
as well as
C_{b}(X)
with the structure of a paired linear
hk
-space, rendering them mutually dual. In
Section

4.2
, a version of the Riesz representation theorem will allow us to identify the elements of
\mathcal{M}(X)
with a certain class of measures which we call
k
-regular measures
. Next, we will construct the monad structure of
\mathcal{M}
and the probability monad
\mathcal{P}
in
Sections

4.3
and
4.4
. Finally, we will show in
Section

4.6
how the formalism of (paired or replete) linear
hk
-spaces can be used to obtain a very coherent theory of vector-valued integration,
monadic vector-valued integration
.

4.1.
The Paired Linear
hk
-space
C_{b}(X)
and its Dual
\mathcal{M}(X)

4.1.1.
The linear
hk
-space
C_{b}(X)

First, we need to define
C_{b}(X)
as a linear
hk
-space. (The
paired
linear structure on
C_{b}(X)
will come later.)

Definition 4.1.1.1
.

We define the linear
hk
-space
C_{b}(X)
of bounded continuous (
\mathbb{K}
-valued) functions on an
hk
-space
X
as the filtered colimit (in the category of
hk
-spaces),

C_{b}(X):=\colim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\>(nD)^{X},

of the diagram,

D^{X}\hookrightarrow(2D)^{X}\hookrightarrow(3D)^{X}\hookrightarrow...,

where

D=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{K}\mid|\lambda|\leq 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{K}

is the unit disk.

Remark 4.1.1.2
.

Since the underlying set of this colimit can be identified with the union of all functions whose absolute value is bounded by some natural number, the elements of
C_{b}(X)
correspond exactly to bounded continuous functions on
X
, justifying the notation. Moreover, (pointwise) addition and scalar multiplication are continuous with respect to the above colimit topology. Hence,
C_{b}(X)
is indeed a linear
hk
-space.

Convergent sequences in
C_{b}(X)
are simple to describe.

Lemma 4.1.1.3
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then a sequence
(f_{n})
of continuous bounded functions converges to
f
in
C_{b}(X)
if, and only if,
(f_{n})
is uniformly bounded and converges in the compact-open topology to
f
.

Proof.

A convergent sequence in
C_{b}(X)
can be identified with a continuous map

\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}\to C_{b}(X)

from the one-point compactification of the natural numbers to
C_{b}(X)
. The image of such map being compact,
2.1.4.13
implies that every convergent sequence lies in one of the spaces
(nD)^{X}
(for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
) and is hence uniformly bounded. Moreover, a sequence converges in
(nD)^{X}
if, and only if, it converges in the compact-open topology (see
2.1.5.1
).
∎

We will need the following lemma on the continuous linear functionals on
C_{b}(X)
later.

Lemma 4.1.1.4
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space and let
\phi
be a (not necessarily continuous) functional on
C_{b}(X)
. Suppose that for every uniformly bounded net of continuous functions
(f_{i})
on
X
converging in the compact-open topology to
0
,
\phi(f_{i})\to 0
. Then
\phi
is continuous.

Proof.

By assumption,
\phi
is continuous on each space of continuous functions
C_{c.o.}(X,nD)
with the compact-open topology, implying that it is also continuous on each
(nD)^{X}
. The claim follows, since
C_{b}(X)
is given as the colimit over these spaces.
∎

4.1.2.
The linear
hk
-space
\mathcal{M}(X)

At this point, as for
C_{b}(X)
, we introduce
\mathcal{M}(X)
only as a linear
hk
-space. Later,
\mathcal{M}(X)
will also be given a paired linear structure, rendering it dual to
C_{b}(X)
. Moreover, while the following definition of
\mathcal{M}(X)
is in terms of functionals, we will later identify
\mathcal{M}(X)
with a space of measures.

Definition 4.1.2.1
.

Define

\mathcal{M}(X):=\overline{\text{span}\{\delta_{x}\in C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}\mid\,x\in X\}}\subseteq C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}

as the closed linear subspace of the natural dual
C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
of
C_{b}(X)
generated by the point evaluations (“Dirac functionals”,
\delta_{x}(f):=f(x)
).

This definition ensures from the start that finitely supported measures will be dense in
\mathcal{M}(X)
(once we have shown how to identify elements of
\mathcal{M}(X)
with measures), a very convenient feature. (For instance, we will use this in the proof of
4.3.2.2
, the commutativity of
\mathcal{M}
as a monad.) Moreover, as a closed linear subspace of a replete linear
hk
-space,
\mathcal{M}(X)
is a replete linear
hk
-space.

4.1.3.
The natural dual of
\mathcal{M}(X)
is
C_{b}(X)

The next lemma will be needed for showing that
\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}\cong C_{b}(X)
, which we will then use to define the paired linear structure on both spaces in an obvious way.

Lemma 4.1.3.1
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then the map

\delta_{\bullet}:X\to\mathcal{M}(X),\;\;x\mapsto\delta_{x}

is continuous.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{M}(X)
was defined as a subspace of
C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
, it suffices to show that the map

X\to C_{b}(X)^{\wedge},\;\;x\mapsto\delta_{\bullet}

is continuous. This map factors as the composite,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(X)^{\wedge}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)|_{C_{b}(X)}}

so its continuity follows from cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
and the fact that the inclusion map
C_{b}(X)\to C(X)
is continuous.
∎

Proposition 4.1.3.2
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then the linear maps,

\Phi:\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}\to C_{b}(X),\;\;\;F\mapsto(x\mapsto F(\delta_{x})),

and,

\Psi:C_{b}(X)\to\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge},\;\;\;f\mapsto(\mu\mapsto\mu(f)),

are continuous and mutually inverse and hence constitute isomorphisms (natural in
X
):

\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}\cong C_{b}(X).

Proof.

Continuity of
\Psi
is clear from cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
, as is continuity of
\Phi
when taking
4.1.3.1
into account.
Now, for any
f\in C_{b}(X)
,
x\in X
,

\displaystyle\Phi\circ\Psi(f)(x)=\Phi(\mu\mapsto\mu(f))(x)

\displaystyle=\delta_{x}(f)=f(x)=\mathsf{id}_{C_{b}(X)}(f)(x).

Similarly, for all
F\in\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}
,
x_{0}\in X
,

\displaystyle\Psi\circ\Phi(F)(\delta_{x_{0}})

\displaystyle=\Psi(x\mapsto F(\delta_{x}))(\delta_{x_{0}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{x_{0}}(x\mapsto F(\delta_{x}))

\displaystyle=F(\delta_{x_{0}})=\mathsf{id}_{\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}}(F)(\delta_{x_{0}}),

which, since the span of all such
\delta_{x_{0}}
is dense in
\mathcal{M}(X)
, implies the claim.
∎

4.1.4.
C_{b}(X)
and
\mathcal{M}(X)
as paired linear
hk
-spaces

Finally, we may now endow
C_{b}(X)
and
\mathcal{M}(X)
with (mutually dual) paired linear structures.

Definition 4.1.4.1
.

We define the paired linear
hk
-space
\mathcal{M}(X)
as equipped with its natural dual,

\mathcal{M}(X)^{*}:=\mathcal{M}(X)^{\wedge}.

Accordingly, we define the paired linear
hk
-space
C_{b}(X)
as being equipped with the admissible dual

C_{b}(X)^{*}:=\mathcal{M}(X).

We hence obtain two functors,

C_{b}(X):hk\mathsf{Top}^{\text{op}}\to\mathsf{L},\;\;\;X\mapsto C_{b}(X),\;f\mapsto(-\circ f),

\mathcal{M}(X):hk\mathsf{Top}\to\mathsf{L},\;\;\;X\mapsto\mathcal{M}(X),\;f\mapsto f_{*}.

4.2.
k
-Regular Measures

4.2.1.
Some measure-theoretic preliminaries

Up to this point, we only know that the spaces
\mathcal{M}(X)
and
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
are spaces of
functionals
, but we really want them to be spaces of genuine
measures
. To finally bring measure theory into the picture, we remind ourselves of some notions from the theory of measures on topological spaces. We follow the terminology of
[
BR07
]
.

Warning 4.2.1.1
.

By default, “measure” will mean “countably additive
\mathbb{K}
-valued measure of bounded variation”.

Definition 4.2.1.2
.

Let
X
be a topological space. The
Baire
\sigma
-algebra
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by all continuous
\mathbb{K}
-valued functions on
X
(it does not matter whether
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{K}=\mathbb{C}
). A
Baire measure
is a measure on the Baire
\sigma
-algebra. Similarly, recall that a
Borel measure
is a measure defined on the
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
, which, by definition, is generated by the open subsets of
X
. A Borel measure
\mu
on
X
is a
Radon measure
if for every
\epsilon>0
, there is a compact subset
K_{\epsilon}\subseteq X
such that
|\mu|(X\mathbin{\backslash}K_{\epsilon})<\epsilon
.

When
X
is sufficiently well-behaved, the notions of Baire and Radon measure essentially coincide:

Theorem 4.2.1.3
.

On a Polish space, the Baire and Borel
\sigma
-algebras agree and every Baire (equivalently Borel) measure is a Radon measure. Moreover, on a
\sigma
-compact completely regular space, every Baire measure has a unique extension to a Radon measure.

Proof.

For the statement about Polish spaces, see
[
BR07
, p. 70, Theorem 7.1.7]
, for the one on compacta, see
[
BR07
, p. 81, Theorem 7.3.4]
.
∎

Hence, if a space
X
is either a Polish or compact, Baire measures on
X
can be identified with Radon measures.

4.2.2.
A Riesz-Markov-type theorem

The proposition that follows is a first step towards our desired representation theorem for
\mathcal{M}(X)
by representing functionals from the larger space
C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
as Baire measures.

Proposition 4.2.2.1
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then for every
\phi\in C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
, there exists a unique Baire measure
\mu
such that

\phi(f)=\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu,

for all
f\in C_{b}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\in C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
. By
[
BR07
, p. 111, Theorem 7.10.1]
, the conclusion of the lemma follows if, and only, if for every monotonically decreasing sequence
(f_{n})
in
C_{b}(X)
converging pointwise to zero,
\phi(f_{n})\to 0
, as well. So let
(f_{n})
be such a sequence. By Dini’s theorem,
(f_{n})
converges to
0
uniformly on compact subsets of
X
. Moreover, since it is monotonically decreasing,
(f_{n})
is uniformly bounded by the constant
\sup_{x\in X}f_{0}(x)
. Hence, by
4.1.1.3
,
(f_{n})
converges to
0
in
C_{b}(X)
and by continuity of
\phi
,
\phi(f_{n})\to 0
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

4.2.3.
k
-Pre-regular measures

The class of measures which represent functionals from
\mathcal{M}(X)
will be referred to as
k
-regular measures
(this will be
4.2.4.1
). As a stepping stone towards this definition and towards understanding this class of measures, we first introduce
k
-pre-regular measures
.

Definition 4.2.3.1
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. A
k
-pre-regular measure
is a Baire measure
\mu
on
X
such that the map

C_{b}(X)\to\mathbb{K},\;\;f\mapsto\int f\mathrm{d}\mu

is continuous.
Denote the set of
k
-pre-regular measures on
X
by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)
.

As an immediate corollary to
4.2.2.1
, we obtain:

Corollary 4.2.3.2
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then the mapping,

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)\to C_{b}(X)^{\wedge},\;\;\;\mu\mapsto\int(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu,

is a bijection, so we may identify functionals in
C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
with
k
-pre-regular measures under this bijection.

Definition 4.2.3.3
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Denote the closed subspace of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)=C(X)^{\wedge}
consisting of
k
-pre-regular
probability
measures (equivalently, positive normalised functionals) by
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)
.

In many cases,
any
Baire measure will be
k
-pre-regular:

Proposition 4.2.3.4
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space and let
\mu
be a Baire measure
X
that is the restriction of some Radon measure
\tilde{\mu}
on
X
to the Baire
\sigma
-algebra. Then
\mu
is a
k
-pre-regular measure. In particular, if
X
either a completely regular
\sigma
-compact
hk
-space (e.g. a compact Hausdorff space, or a Brauner space), or a Polish space, then
every
Baire measure is a
k
-pre-regular measure.

Proof.

Let
(f_{i})
be a net of continuous functions on
X
uniformly bounded by
C>0
converging uniformly on compact subsets to
0
. By
4.1.1.4
, it suffices to show that

\int f_{i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\to 0.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Then, since
\tilde{\mu}
is a Radon measure, there exists a compact subset
K
such that
|\tilde{\mu}|(X\mathbin{\backslash}K)<\epsilon
. Now, by the compact convergence of
(f_{i})
, there is an index
i_{0}
such that for all
i\geq i_{0}
,

\sup_{x\in K}|f_{i}(x)|<\epsilon.

Now,

\displaystyle\Big|\int f_{i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\Big|\leq\int|f_{i}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}|\tilde{\mu}|

\displaystyle=\int_{K}|f_{i}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}|\tilde{\mu}|+\int_{X\mathbin{\backslash}K}|f_{i}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}|\tilde{\mu}|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in K}|f_{i}(x)||\tilde{\mu}|(K)+C|\tilde{\mu}|(X\mathbin{\backslash}K)

\displaystyle<(|\mu|(X)+C)\,\epsilon.

This shows convergence and, in conclusion, that
\mu
is
k
-pre-regular. Now, if
X
is completely regular
\sigma
-compact
hk
-space or a Polish space, then every Baire measure admits a unique extension to a Radon measure (see
4.2.1.3
), so that indeed, every Baire measure is
k
-pre-regular.
∎

4.2.4.
k
-Regular measures

The space of
k
-pre-regular measures is still too large – it corresponds to
C_{b}(X)^{\wedge}
instead of the possibly smaller paired linear
hk
-space dual
C_{b}(X)^{*}=\mathcal{M}(X)
. As a first step, the notion of
k
-regular measure
solves this problem trivially by
requiring
integration against a
k
-regular measure to define a functional in
\mathcal{M}(X)
(so that the question becomes finding classes of examples of
k
-regular measures that include the practically relevant ones).

Definition 4.2.4.1
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. A
k
-regular measure
on
X
is a
k
-pre-regular measure
\mu
such that,

\int(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\in C_{b}(X)^{*}(=\mathcal{M}(X)).

As a consequence of this definition and
4.2.3.2
, we may identify
k
-regular measures with the elements of
\mathcal{M}(X)
and will therefore use the same notation
\mathcal{M}(X)
also for the set of
k
-regular measures on
X
. Similarly, we will write

\mathcal{P}(X):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{M}(X)\mid\mu\geq 0,\>\mu(1)=1\}

for the closed subspace of
\mathcal{M}(X)
consisting of positive, normalised functionals. In other words,
\mathcal{P}(X)
is, under the identification of measures and functionals, the space of
k
-regular probability measures on
X
.

The pressing question is now, of course, whether the class of
k
-regular measures is wide enough to encompass those examples which appear in applications. As we will see, arbitrary Baire probability measures on compact Hausdorff spaces are
k
-regular, and the same result holds for Polish spaces. This will require examining the question of convergence in
\mathcal{M}(X)
and how it relates to the more familiar notion of
weak convergence
of measures.

4.2.5.
Convergence in spaces of
k
-regular measures

Weak convergence of measures

The following should be a familiar definition:

Definition 4.2.5.1
.

Recall that a net
(\mu_{i})
of measures on a topological space
X
is said to
converge weakly
to
\mu
if for all
f\in C_{b}(X)
,

\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}\to\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu.

When
(\mu_{i})
is the sequence of distributions
(P_{x_{i}})
of some random variables
(x_{i})
, weak convergence of
(P_{x_{i}})
is exactly
convergence in distribution
of the
(x_{i})
in the sense of probability theory.

The simplest case in which convergence of
sequences
in
\mathcal{M}(X)
(and of general nets in
\mathcal{P}(X)
) coincides with weak convergence is when
X
is compact. In this case, the notion of
k
-regular measure reduces to that of Baire measure.

Proposition 4.2.5.2
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space. Then every Baire measure on
X
is
k
-regular (so that
\mathcal{M}(X)
consists exactly of the Baire measures on
X
). Moreover, the topology of
\mathcal{P}(X)
coincides with the topology of weak convergence of measures, and
\mathcal{P}(X)
is a compact Hausdorff space. In particular, a net of measures converges in
\mathcal{M}(X)
if, and only if, it converges weakly.

Proof.

When
X
is compact,
C_{b}(X)=C(X)
is the
Banach
space of continuous functions on
X
and using Smith duality (
2.3.0.1
) together with
4.1.3.2
, we see that

\mathcal{M}(X)=C(X)^{\wedge}.

The left hand side is the space of
k
-regular measures, while the right hand side can be identified with the space of Baire (equivalently, Radon, by
4.2.1.3
) measures on
X
(by the Riesz representation theorem, see
[
BR07
, Theorem 7.10.4]
), so every Baire measure on
X
is indeed
k
-regular. As a closed subspace of the compact unit ball of the space
\mathcal{M}(X)=C(X)^{\wedge}
,
\mathcal{P}(X)
is compact and its topology coincides with the weak-
*
topology, i.e. the topology of weak convergence of measures (see
2.2.3.4
).
∎

Uniformly tight families and Prokhorov spaces

We will subsequently need the following measure-theoretic concepts.

Definition 4.2.5.3
.

Recall that a family of Radon measures
(\mu_{i})
on an
hk
-space
X
is
uniformly tight
if for every
\epsilon>0
there is a compact set
K
such that
|\mu|(X\mathbin{\backslash}K)<\epsilon
.
X
is a
strongly sequentially Prokhorov space
if every weakly convergent sequence of Radon measures on
X
is uniformly tight.

When does convergence in
\mathcal{P}(X)
reduce to weak convergence?

Theorem 4.2.5.4
.

Let
X
be a either a Polish space or a completely regular hemicompact
hk
-space. Then a sequence
(\mu_{n})
of probability measures converges in
\mathcal{P}(X)
if, and only if, it converges weakly.

Proof.

Only the “if” direction is non-trivial, so suppose that
\mu_{n}\to\mu
weakly. We want to show that
\mu_{n}\to\mu
uniformly on compact subsets of
C_{b}(X)
. So let
L\subseteq C_{b}(X)
be compact and
\epsilon>0
. The hypotheses imply that every Baire measure
\nu
on
X
extends uniquely to a Radon measure
\tilde{\nu}
(see
4.2.1.3
) and that
X
is strongly sequentially Prokhorov (see
[
BR07
, p. 220, Proposition 8.20.12]
for the case of hemicompact
hk
-spaces, and
[
BR07
, p. 206, Theorem 8.6.8]
for the case of Polish spaces). Therefore, the family
\{|\tilde{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{\mu}|\}
is uniformly tight and there exists a compact subset
K_{\epsilon}\subseteq X
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
|\tilde{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{\mu}|(X\mathbin{\backslash}K_{\epsilon})<\epsilon
. Since the family of functions
L
is compact, it is uniformly bounded by some constant
C>0
. Moreover, given the hypotheses, the restrictions
\tilde{\mu}_{n}|_{K_{\epsilon}},\tilde{\mu}|_{K_{\epsilon}}
are Radon measures on the compact Hausdorff space
K_{\epsilon}
, and
\tilde{\mu}_{n}|_{K_{\epsilon}}\to\tilde{\mu}|_{K_{\epsilon}}
weakly and hence uniformly on compact subsets of
C(K_{\epsilon})
(see
4.2.5.2
). The image of
L
under restriction of functions to
K_{\epsilon}
,

L|_{K_{\epsilon}}:=\{f|_{K_{\epsilon}}\mid f\in L\},

is compact, since it is the image of the compact set
L
under continuous map
f\mapsto f\circ\iota_{K_{\epsilon}}
, where
\iota_{K_{\epsilon}}:K_{\epsilon}\to X
is the inclusion map. By compact convergence of
\tilde{\mu}_{n}|_{K_{\epsilon}}
, we have that for sufficiently large
n
,

\sup_{f\in L|_{K_{\epsilon}}}\Big|\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{n}|_{K_{\epsilon}}-\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}|_{K_{\epsilon}}\Big|<\epsilon.

Putting all of these observations together, we obtain that for all
f\in L
and sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}-\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{X\mathbin{\backslash}K_{\epsilon}}|f|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}|\tilde{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{\mu}|+\Big|\int_{K_{\epsilon}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{n}-\int_{K_{\epsilon}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{n}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon+\Big|\int_{K_{\epsilon}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}_{n}|_{K_{\epsilon}}-\int_{K_{\epsilon}}f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mu}|_{K_{\epsilon}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq(C+1)\,\epsilon,

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Theorem 4.2.5.5
.

Let
V
be a separable Fréchet space. Then the Borel and Baire
\sigma
-algebras on the natural dual
V^{\wedge}
coincide and every Baire measure on
V^{\wedge}
is a Radon measure and
k
-regular. Hence,
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
consists exactly of the Radon probability measures on
V^{\wedge}
. Moreover, a sequence of probability measures in
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
converges if, and only if, it converges weakly. Finally,
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
is a QCB space (in particular, a sequential space) and consequently, convergence of sequences of probability measures completely characterises the topology of
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
.

Proof.

The natural dual
V^{\wedge}
of
V
is a separable Brauner space and therefore in particular a hemicompact QCB space.
Since
V^{\wedge}
is a topological vector space, it is also completely regular and by
4.2.1.3
, every Baire measure has a unique extension to a Radon measure. Furthermore, since
V^{\wedge}
is a QCB space, it is hereditarily Lindelöf (see
2.1.6.5
), so by
[
BR07
, p. 13, Corollary 6.3.5]
, the Baire and Borel sigma algebras coincide. Therefore, every Baire measure on
V^{\wedge}
is a Radon measure.

By
4.2.3.4
, every Baire measure on
V^{\wedge}
is a
k
-preregular measure. To show that, moreover, every Baire (equivalently, Radon) measure on
V^{\wedge}
is
k
-regular, it suffices to show that for every Radon measure
\mu
on
V^{\wedge}
, there is a sequence of finitely supported measures converging to
\mu
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(V^{\wedge})=C_{b}(V^{\wedge})^{\wedge}
. Since, by
4.2.5.4
, convergence in
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
is equivalent to weak convergence and
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(V^{\wedge})
is closed, it even suffices to show that for every Radon measure
\mu
on
V^{\wedge}
there is a sequence of finitely supported measures converging weakly to
\mu
. So let
\mu
be a Radon measure on
V^{\wedge}
.

Let
K_{n}\subseteq V^{\wedge}
be an increasing sequence of compact subsets such that

V^{\wedge}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K_{n}.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the restriction
\mu|_{K_{n}}
is a Radon measure on
K_{n}
. By
4.2.5.2
, there exists a finitely supported measure
\nu_{n}
on
K_{n}
such that for all
f\in C_{b}(V^{\wedge})
,

\left|\int_{K_{n}}f\>\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\nu_{n}-\mu)\right|<\frac{1}{n}.

We claim that the sequence
(\nu_{n})
converges weakly to
\mu
. Let
\epsilon>0
and choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|\mu|(V^{\wedge}\mathbin{\backslash}K_{n})<\epsilon
and
1/N<\epsilon
. Then for all
n\geq N
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{V^{\wedge}}f\>\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\nu_{n}-\mu)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{V^{\wedge}\mathbin{\backslash}K_{n}}f\>\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\nu_{n}-\mu)\right|+\left|\int_{K_{n}}f\>\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\nu_{n}-\mu)\right|

\displaystyle<\|f\|_{\sup}\,\epsilon+\epsilon,

proving the claim that
\nu_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu
, which is hence a
k
-regular measure.

Finally, the fact that
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
is a QCB space follows from the fact that QCB spaces are closed under the formation of countable (co-)limits and exponentials (i.e. spaces of continuous maps).
∎

4.2.6.
The Bochner-Minlos-Lévy-Fernique homeomorphism

Let us point out that, in conjunction with cartesian closure and sequentiality of QCB spaces,
4.2.5.5
allows for a very concise simultaneous formulation of the Bochner-Minlos and Lévy-Fernique theorems: if
V
is a separable nuclear Fréchet space, then the Fourier transform is a homeomorphism between
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
and the space of continuous normalised positive-definite functions on
V
. Let us make this more precise.

Definition 4.2.6.1
.

Let
V
be a linear
hk
-space. Following
[
BR07
, Definition 7.13.3]
, a continuous function
f:V\to\mathbb{C}
is
positive-definite
if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in V
,

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{i}\overline{c_{j}}f(x_{i}-x_{j})\geq 0.

We say that
f
is
normalised
if
f(0)=1
.
Finally, we denote the set of continuous normalised positive-definite functions on
V
by
C_{1}^{+}(V)
and endow
C_{1}^{+}(V)
with the subspace topology that it inherits from
C(V)
as a closed subspace.

Definition 4.2.6.2
.

Let
V
be a linear
hk
-space and let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
be a probability measure. The
characteristic function
of
\mu
is the function
\widehat{\mu}:V\to\mathbb{C}
given by,

\widehat{\mu}(x):=\int_{V^{\wedge}}e^{2\pi i\phi(x)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\phi).\qquad(x\in V)

The map
\mu\mapsto\widehat{\mu}
is called the
Fourier transform
.

With these definitions in place, we may formulate:

Theorem 4.2.6.3
.

Let
V
be a separable nuclear Fréchet space (for example,
V=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
). Then the Fourier transform defines a
homeomorphism
,

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})\to C_{1}^{+}(V),\;\;\mu\mapsto\int_{V^{\wedge}}e^{2\pi i\phi(x)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(\phi).

Proof.

The statement that
\mathcal{F}
is a well-defined bijection is (a special case of) the Bochner-Minlos theorem (see
[
Mey
, Théorème 1]
), taking into account that by
4.2.5.5
,
\mathcal{P}(V^{\wedge})
consists exactly of the Radon measures on
V^{\wedge}
. That
\mathcal{F}
is continuous follows from the fact that integration of continuous bounded functions against
k
-regular measure is continuous (by definition) and the cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
. Finally, as a closed subspace of a QCB space
C(V)
,
C_{1}^{+}(V)
is a QCB space (see
2.1.6.4
). In particular,
C_{1}^{+}(V)
is a sequential space. Therefore, the continuity of the inverse Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}^{-1}
is equivalent to its sequential continuity, and this follows from the Lévy-Fernique continuity theorem (
[
Mey
, Théorème 2]
).
∎

Remark 4.2.6.4
.

[
Mey
, Théorème 2]
also entails that a sequence converges in
C_{1}^{+}(V)
if, and only if, it converges pointwise to a function that is continuous at the origin.

4.2.7.
k
-Regular measures on Polish spaces

Our final observation concerning
k
-regular measures is that on Polish spaces, their theory completely reduces to the classical one.

Proposition 4.2.7.1
.

Let
X
be a Polish space. Then following
every
Baire measure is a
k
-regular measure and hence, the notions of Baire, Radon and
k
-regular measure all coincide (see
4.2.1.3
). Moreover, the topology on
\mathcal{P}(X)
is given by the topology of weak convergence of measures and
\mathcal{P}(X)
is again a Polish space.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be a Baire (equivalently, Radon, see
4.2.1.3
) probability measure on
X
. By
4.2.3.4
,
\mu
is
k
-pre-regular. Now, let
\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)
be the space of Radon measures with the topology of weak convergence, and recall that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)
is the space of
k
-pre-regular probability measures with the subspace topology. As sets,
\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}
(again, by
4.2.3.4
). We want to show that the respective topologies coincide, as well. First, note that the identity map
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)\to\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)
is continuous, since a convergent net in
\mathcal{P}(X)
is also weakly convergent. By
[
BR07
, p. 213, Theorem 8.9.4]
, our assumption that
X
is a Polish space implies that
\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)
is a Polish space, too. Hence, sequential continuity of the identity map
\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)
(which is provided by
4.2.5.4
) implies continuity, so the two topologies coincide and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)=\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)
also as topological spaces. Finally, finitely supported measures are dense in
\mathcal{P}_{w}(X)
(see
[
BR07
, p. 213, Theorem 8.9.4 (ii)]
) and therefore

\mathcal{P}(X)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{pre}}(X)=\mathcal{P}_{w}(X),

which completes the proof.
∎

The above guarantees that most probability measures that occur in applications are
k
-regular. (For a concrete example, take the law of a standard Brownian motion, understood as Baire probability measure on the separable Fréchet space
C([0,\infty),\mathbb{R}^{n})
.)

4.3.
\mathcal{M}
as a commutative monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}

4.3.1.
The monad structure of
\mathcal{M}

We now describe the monad structure of
\mathcal{M}
. Unit and multiplication are given as follows.

Definition 4.3.1.1
.

Define the (families of) map(s),

\delta_{\bullet}:X\mapsto\mathcal{M}(X),\;\;x\mapsto\delta_{x},\;\;\;\;\;(X\in hk\mathsf{Top})

and

\smallint{}:\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X))\to\mathcal{M}(X),\smallint{}(\pi)(f):=\int_{\mathcal{M(X)}}\mu(f)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi(\mu).\;\;\;\;\;(X\in hk\mathsf{Top})

Note that under the identification of measures with functionals, we have that

(4.1)

\smallint{}(\pi)(f)=\pi(\mu\mapsto\mu(f)).\qquad(\pi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X)),f\in C_{b}(X))

Lemma 4.3.1.2
.

The families of maps
\delta_{\bullet}
and
\smallint{}
constitute well-defined natural transformations.

Proof.

1.

The map
\delta_{\bullet}
is well-defined and continuous:
This was
4.1.3.1
.

2.

Naturality of
\delta_{\bullet}
:
We need to show that for all
hk
-spaces
X,Y
and every continuous map
f:X\to Y
the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

But this just means that for all
x\in X
,

f_{*}\delta_{x}=\delta_{f(x)},

which holds.

3.

The map
\smallint{}
is well-defined and continuous:
First notice that
\smallint{}
is well-defined, since for every
f\in C_{b}(X)
, the map
\mu\mapsto\mu(f)
is continuous and bounded (by
\|f\|_{\sup}\cdot|\mu|(X)
) on
\mathcal{M}(X)
. Moreover, being given by a certain evaluation-type map (see
Eq.

4.1
),
\smallint{}
is continuous by cartesian closure of
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

4.

The map
\smallint{}
is natural:
We need to show that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(Y))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(Y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{*})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smallint{}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\smallint{}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

To prove this, we calculate, using
Eq.

4.1
, that for all
\pi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X))
and all
h\in C_{b}(X)
,

\displaystyle f_{*}\left(\smallint{}(\pi)\right)(h)

\displaystyle=\smallint{}(\pi)(h\circ f)

\displaystyle=\pi(\mu\mapsto\mu(h\circ f))

\displaystyle=\pi(\mu\mapsto f_{*}\mu(h))

\displaystyle=\pi(f_{*}\circ(\mu\mapsto\mu(h)))

\displaystyle=(f_{*})_{*}\pi(\mu\mapsto\mu(h))

\displaystyle=\smallint{}((f_{*})_{*}\pi)(h).

∎

Theorem 4.3.1.3
.

(\mathcal{M},\delta_{\bullet},\smallint{})
is a monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

Proof.

We need to verify the monad laws, which amounts to the following three calculations, where
X
is an
hk
-space,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
,
f\in C_{b}(X)
and
\pi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X))
.

1.

First unit law:

\smallint{}(\delta_{\mu})(f)=\int\nu(f)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\delta_{\mu}(\nu)=\mu(f).

2.

Second unit law:

(\smallint{}(\delta_{\bullet})_{*}\mu)(f)=\int\delta_{x}(f)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu=\mu(f)

3.

Associativity law: We need to show that for all
\Pi\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{M}(X)))
and all
h\in C_{b}(X)
,

\smallint{}\left(\smallint{}(\Pi)\right)(h)=\smallint{}\left(\smallint{}\right)_{*}\Pi.

To do so, we repeatedly use
Eq.

4.1
to obtain,

\displaystyle\smallint{}\left(\smallint{}(\Pi)\right)(h)

\displaystyle=\left(\smallint{}(\Pi)\right)(\mu\mapsto\mu(h))

\displaystyle=\Pi(\nu\mapsto\nu(\mu\mapsto\mu(h)))

\displaystyle=\Pi(\nu\mapsto\left(\smallint{}(\nu)\right)(h))

\displaystyle=\Pi\left([\mu\mapsto\mu(h)]\circ\smallint{}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\smallint{}\right)_{*}\Pi(\mu\mapsto\mu(h))

\displaystyle=\smallint{}\left(\smallint{}\right)_{*}\Pi,

completing the proof.

∎

4.3.2.
Product measures (the commutative monad structure of
\mathcal{M}
)

Definition 4.3.2.1
.

Let
X,Y
be
hk
-spaces and let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}(X)
,
\nu\in\mathcal{M}(Y)
be
k
-regular measures. Define the product measure
\mu\otimes\nu\in\mathcal{M}(X\times Y)
on
X\times Y
as (the
k
-regular measure representing the functional),

(\mu\otimes\nu)(f):=\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y).\;\;\;\;\;(f\in C_{b}(X\times Y))

The family of maps

\otimes:\mathcal{M}(X)\times\mathcal{M}(Y)\to\mathcal{M}(X\times Y),\;\;(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\mu\otimes\nu,\;\;\;\;((X,Y)\in hk\mathsf{Top}\times hk\mathsf{Top})

is now a natural transformation (this follows from a change of variables in the definition) and we have that:

Theorem 4.3.2.2
.

(\mathcal{M},\delta_{\bullet},\smallint{},\otimes)
is a commutative monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

Proof.

We need to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(X)\times\mathcal{M}(Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(X\times Y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(Y)\times\mathcal{M}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}(Y\times X)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{*}}

commutes (where
\sigma
is the braiding), which amounts to showing that for all
f\in C_{b}(X)
,

\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\otimes\nu=\int f\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma_{*}(\nu\otimes\mu).

After a change of variables and expanding, this becomes,

(4.2)

\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)=\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

We might now be tempted to invoke a version of Fubini’s theorem for signed measures, but let us take the oppurtunity to give an independent proof which will then instead
entail
this version of Fubini’s theorem.

Note that both sides of (
4.2
) are continuous in
(\mu,\nu)
and that equality holds when
\mu
and
\nu
are finitely supported (since then, (
4.2
) reduces to an interchange of finite sums). The claim now follows from density of finitely supported measures in
\mathcal{M}(X\times Y)
.
∎

4.4.
The Probability Monad
\mathcal{P}
on the Category of
hk
-Spaces

Because the pushforward of a probability measure remains a probability measure,
\mathcal{P}(-)
inherits the structure of a functor from
\mathcal{M}
. Similarly, we can equip
\mathcal{P}
with a unit and multiplication coming from the monad structure of
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 4.4.0.1
.

Define the family of continuous maps,

\delta_{\bullet}:X\mapsto\mathcal{P}(X),\;\;x\mapsto\delta_{x},\;\;\;\;\;(X\in hk\mathsf{Top})

as well as,

\smallint{}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(X))\to\mathcal{P}(X),\smallint{}(\pi)(f):=\int_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\mu(f)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\pi(\mu).\;\;\;\;\;(X\in hk\mathsf{Top})

Note that this definition is exactly the same as
4.3.1.1
if we replace
\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, when
\mu
and
\nu
probability measures then so is the product measure
\mu\otimes\nu
from
4.3.2.1
and we obtain a family of maps

\otimes:\mathcal{P}(X)\times\mathcal{P}(Y)\to\mathcal{P}(X\times Y),\;\;(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\mu\otimes\nu,\;\;\;\;((X,Y)\in hk\mathsf{Top}\times hk\mathsf{Top})

We can therefore, the proof being exactly as before, conclude:

Theorem 4.4.0.2
.

The families
\delta_{\bullet}
,
\smallint{}
and
\otimes
constitute natural transformations and
(\mathcal{P},\delta_{\bullet},\smallint{},\otimes)
is a commutative monad on
hk\mathsf{Top}
.

Remark 4.4.0.3
.

Now that we know that
\mathcal{P}
is a monad, we may rephrase the results of
4.2.5.2
and
4.2.7.1
as saying that
\mathcal{P}
restricts to the Giry monad on Polish spaces, and to the Radon monad on compact Hausdorff spaces, recovering (and in some sense “interpolating” between) two well-known probability monads. Moreover, since the category
h\mathsf{QCB}
of
k
-Hausdorff QCB spaces is closed under the formation of limits and exponentials in
hk\mathsf{Top}
,
\mathcal{P}
also restricts to a probability monad on
h\mathsf{QCB}
.

4.5.
The Free Paired Linear
hk
-Space
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
as a Space of Compactly Supported Measures

In Chapter 3, we defined
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
to be the free paired linear
hk
-space (equivalently, the free replete linear
hk
-space) on
X
(see
3.3.5.15
). We now justify this notation by showing that
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
can be identified with with the space of compactly supported
k
-regular measures on
X
. In contrast to
\mathcal{M}(X)
, of which we needed to show “by hand” that it defines a commutative monad, this is automatic for
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
from the general considerations of Chapter 3 (see
Remark

3.3.5.17
).

Let us start by observing that we may regard
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
as a subset of
\mathcal{M}(X)
.

Lemma 4.5.0.1
.

The map

i^{*}:\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)\to\mathcal{M}(X)

obtained as the adjoint of the inclusion map
i:C_{b}(X)\to C(X)
is injective.

Proof.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
and suppose that
i^{*}(\mu)=i^{*}(\nu)
. Then, for all
f\in C_{b}(X)
,
\nu(f)=\mu(f)
. Since
C_{b}(X)
is dense in
C(X)
, this implies that
\mu=\nu
.
∎

Definition 4.5.0.2
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. By a compactly supported
k
-regular measure on
X
we mean a
k
-regular measure
\mu\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}(X)
which is the pushforward of a Baire measure defined on a compact subspace of
X
under the inclusion. (Of course, by
4.2.5.2
,
k
-regularity of
\mu
