is automatic in this case.) We denote the set of compactly supported
k
-regular measures by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)
.

Remark 4.5.0.3
.

Why do we not just define compactly supported
k
-regular measures as “
k
-regular measures whose support is compact”? This is for a technical reason: Remember that on a general topological space, the Baire
\sigma
-algebra may be a proper subset of the Borel
\sigma
-algebra, which is why the usual definition of the support of a
Radon
(or, more generally,
\tau
-additive
) measure cannot be applied here.

However, if
X
is a
Hausdorff

hk
-space, then every tight (in particular, every compactly supported
k
-regular) Baire measure on
X
admits a unique extension to a Radon measure
[
BR07
, Theorem 7.3.10]
. Hence, for all practical purposes, the set of compactly supported
k
-regular measures can be identified with the set of compactly supported Radon measures.

Before we can provide an identification between compactly supported measures in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)
and functionals in
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
, as promised, we need a lemma.

Lemma 4.5.0.4
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space and let
\phi
be a continuous linear functional on the space
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)
of continuous functions
with the compact-open topology
. Then there exists a compactly supported
k
-regular measure
\mu
on
X
such that for all
f\in C(X)
,

\phi(f)=\int_{X}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Proof.

Put

P:=\prod_{K\subseteq X\text{ compact}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{}^{\mathsf{Top}}\;\;C(K),

where
\prod^{\mathsf{Top}}
denotes the product in
\mathsf{Top}
(equivalently, in the category of locally convex topological vector spaces).
Since
X
is
k
-Hausdorff, the compact-open topology is the topology of uniform convergence on compact subsets and hence, the map

C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)\hookrightarrow P,\;\;\;f\mapsto(f|_{K})

is an embedding of locally convex topological vector spaces. By the Hahn-Banach extension theorem,
\phi
has an extension
\tilde{\phi}
to
P
. Every linear functional on a product of locally convex topological vector spaces is a finite sum of functionals factoring through one of its factors, so there exist
K_{1},\dots,K_{n}\subseteq X
compact and
\phi_{1}\in C(K_{1})^{\wedge},\dots\phi_{n}\in C(K_{n})^{\wedge}
such that

\tilde{\phi}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}\circ\pi_{i},

where
\pi_{i}:P\to C(K_{i})
is the canonical projection. By the Riesz–Markov–Kakutani theorem for the case of compact Hausdorff spaces, there exist Baire measures
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}
on
K_{1},\dots,K_{n}
such that

\phi_{i}=\int_{K_{i}}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{i},

for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Hence, we obtain that

\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{K_{i}}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}.

Let
\iota_{i}:K_{i}\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion and let

\mu:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\iota_{i})_{*}\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X).

Then we have that

\phi=\int_{X}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu,

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Theorem 4.5.0.5
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space. Then the map

\Phi:\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)\to\mathcal{M}_{c}(X),\;\;\;\mu\mapsto\int_{X}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu

is a well-defined natural bijection between the set
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)
of compactly supported
k
-regular measures on
X
and the set
C(X)^{*}\cong\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
. In particular, when
X
is Hausdorff
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
can be identified with the set of compactly supported Radon measures on
X
(see
Remark

4.5.0.3
).

Proof.

1.

\Phi
is well-defined:

1.1.

\Phi(\mu)
is continuous on
C(X)
(for any
\mu\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)
:
it is even continuous on
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)
, since

\Big|\int_{X}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big|\leq\mu(X)\,\sup_{x\in K}|f(x)|,

for all
f\in C(X)
, where
K:=\supp\mu
is the support of
\mu
.

1.2.

\im\Phi\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
:
Let
\mu\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{c}(X)
. We want to show that

\tilde{\mu}:=\int_{X}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X).

Let
K:=\supp\mu
. Since
K
is compact,
\mathcal{M}_{c}(K)=C(K)^{\wedge}
. Now,

\tilde{\mu}|_{K}:=\int_{K}(-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(K)=C(K)^{\wedge}.

Let
\iota:K\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. Then

\tilde{\mu}=\iota_{*}(\tilde{\mu}|_{K})\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X).

2.

\Phi
is injective:
Since
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
can be identified with a subspace of
\mathcal{M}(X)
(
4.5.0.1
), we already know that
\Phi
is injective from
4.2.2.1
.

3.

\Phi
is surjective:
As
\Phi
has dense image (since linear combinations of point masses are dense in
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
), it suffices to show that
\im\Phi\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
is closed.
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
is a closed subspace of
C(C(X))
, by definition, and a closed subset of
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C(X))
is also closed in
C(C(X))
. It is therefore sufficient to prove that
\im\Phi
is closed as a subset of
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C(X))
. Moreover,
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X))
is a subspace (in the sense of carrying the subspace topology) of
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C(X))
because the compact subsets of
C(X)
and
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)
coincide. This in turn reduces the claim to showing that
\im\Phi
is closed in
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X))
, which follows from the fact that
\im\Phi
consists precisely of those continuous functions in
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X))
which are linear, by
4.5.0.4
.

∎

4.6.
Monadic Vector-Valued Integration

4.6.1.
The vector-valued integral

We now show how the preceding developments can be applied to obtain a particularly simple and coherent theory of vector-valued integration. Implementing a general idea of Kock
[
Koc11
]
which was refined and further developed by Lucyshyn-Wright in his doctoral thesis
[
LW13
]
(see there for further references), we realise our vector valued integral by viewing paired linear
hk
-spaces as modules over the commutative monad
\mathcal{M}_{c}
. All the desired properties of the resulting vector-valued integral follow naturally from this, without much further work, which is the reason for the term
monadic vector-valued integration
. In this setting, the two notational components of the integral, the integral sign
\smallint{}
and what comes after it (“
f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)
”), both have separate precise meanings which we now define.

Definition 4.6.1.1
.

Let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space. Since
\mathcal{M}_{c}(V)
is the free paired linear
hk
-space on
V
, the identity
V\to V
extends uniquely to a morphism
\mathcal{M}_{c}(V)\to V
of paired linear
hk
-spaces. We denote this map by

\smallint{}:\mathcal{M}_{c}(V)\to V\;\;\;\;\;\text{or}\;\;\;\;\int:\mathcal{M}_{c}(V)\to V

Moreover, let us introduce the following notation for the pushforward:

f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x):=f_{*}\mu.

That this definition agrees with the usual Lebesgue integral in the scalar case is guaranteed by the following fact.

Proposition 4.6.1.2
.

Let
f:\mathbb{K}\to\mathbb{K}
be continuous and let
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(\mathbb{K})
. Then,

\smallint{}(f_{*}\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{K}}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{K}}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

where the right hand side denotes a Lebesgue integral in the usual sense, and the
\smallint{}
on the left hand side denotes the map,

\smallint{}:\mathcal{M}_{c}(\mathbb{K})\to\mathbb{K},\;\;\nu\mapsto\nu(\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{K}}),

from
4.6.1.1
.

Proof.

This is simply the change-of-variables formula for the Lebesgue integral:

\int_{\mathbb{K}}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=\int_{\mathbb{K}}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}f_{*}\mu(x)=(f_{*}\mu)(\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{K}})=\smallint{}(f_{*}\mu).

∎

Remark 4.6.1.3
.

We restrict our attention to integration of continuous maps against compactly supported measures for two reasons. On the one hand, this is the setting which naturally arises from the monad structure of
\mathcal{M}_{c}
, allowing us to contemplate the phenomena specific to this monadic vector-valued integration theory.
On the other hand, this is a sufficient level of generality for many important cases of vector-valued integration such as the integration of continuous curves (which is certainly covered by this setting). For example, integrals of continuous curves are crucial in infinite-dimensional calculus (see Part I of
[
Ham82
]
), as well as the theory of vector-valued holomorphic functions (see
[
Gar18
, Chapter 15]
).

Remark 4.6.1.4
.

We can now interpret the (
hk\mathsf{Top}
-enriched) free-forgetful adjunction between paired linear
hk
-spaces and
hk
-spaces (see Chapter 3,
Remark

3.3.5.16
) in terms of vector-valued integration as follows. Consider for any
hk
-space
X
and paired linear
hk
-space
V
the map

I:C(X,V)\to[\mathcal{M}_{c}(X),V].\;\;f\mapsto\left(\mu\mapsto\int f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\right)

For each
f\in C(X,V)
, the morphism
I(f)
makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute, since
I(f)(\delta_{x})=\smallint{}(f_{*}\delta_{x})=f(x)
. By the uniqueness of such morphism,
I
is exactly the natural homeomorphism,

I:C(X,V)\to[\mathcal{M}_{c}(X),V],

constituting the free-forgetful adjunction between paired linear
hk
-spaces and
hk
-spaces.

The change-of-variables formula

Functoriality of
\mathcal{M}_{c}
immediately yields the change-of vairables formula for the vector-valued integral:

Proposition 4.6.1.5
.

Let
X,Y
be
hk
-spaces, let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space and let
f:Y\to V
,
g:X\to Y
be continuous maps. Then:

f(g(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{*}\mu.

Proof.

By definition, and functoriality of
\mathcal{M}_{c}
,

f(g(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=(f\circ g)_{*}\mu=f_{*}(g_{*}\mu)=f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}g_{*}\mu.

∎

Interchange with linear operators

We may freely interchange integral signs and morphisms of paired linear
hk
-spaces:

Proposition 4.6.1.6
.

Let
X
be an
hk
-space and let
f:V\to W
be a morphism of paired linear
hk
-spaces. Then for every continuous map
g:X\to V
and every
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
,

f\Big(\int g(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)=\int f(g(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

Proof.

First note that the claim holds when
\mu=\delta_{x_{0}}
is a Dirac delta measure at
x_{0}\in X
. The general case now follows from the fact that
f
is linear and continuous, and that linear combinations of Dirac measures are dense in
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
.
∎

Continuous bilinearity

One feature of the present theory that needs to be emphasised is that the integration pairing,

\int:C(X,V)\times\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)\to V,\;\;(f,\mu)\mapsto\int f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

is continuous, which is a direct consequence of cartesian closure together with the continuity of
f\mapsto f_{*}
, and crucially depends on this setup. If were considering
C(X,V)
and
\mathcal{M}_{c}(X)
as locally convex topological vector spaces, and used the
\mathsf{Top}
-product
\times_{\mathsf{Top}}
, this could not possibly hold (see
Section

1.3.4
).

4.6.2.
A vector-valued Fubini theorem

Associativity of the integral

We would like to be able to form double integrals and formulate a Fubini theorem. However, the term

\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y),

which representing in such double integral is
a priori
ambiguous in our notation, as this double integral could –
a priori
– have two distinct interpretations, depending on parentheses. That a rearrangement of such parentheses does not change the result might be called the
associativity
of the integral (a fact, which, interestingly, does not seem to have a “classical” counterpart as it relies on an independent precise meaning of expressions of the form “
(f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)
”).

Proposition 4.6.2.1

(Associativity of integration)
.

Let
X,Y
be
hk
-spaces and let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space. Let
f:X\times Y\to V
be a continuous map,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(X),\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(Y)
. Then

(4.3)

\int\Big(\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)=\int\Big(\int\Big((f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\Big)\Big)

Therefore, we may drop parentheses in these expressions all together, simply writing

\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)

for either side of equation (
4.3
).

Proof.

We simply rewrite,

\displaystyle\int\Big(\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)

\displaystyle=\int[\smallint{}\circ(f(x,-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x))]_{*}\nu

\displaystyle=\int((\smallint{})_{*}\circ(f(x,-)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x))_{*})(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int(\smallint{})_{*}(f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y))

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\int\Big(f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)\Big)\Big).

∎

Commutativity of the integral (Fubini)

With unambiguous, associative double integrals in place, we now come to a vector-valued Fubini theorem.

Proposition 4.6.2.2
.

Let
X,Y
be
hk
-spaces and let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space. Let
f:X\times Y\to V
be a continuous map,
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}X,\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{c}(Y)
. Then:

\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)=\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu(y)=\int\int f(x,y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}(\mu\otimes\nu)(x,y).

Proof.

Clearly, the identity holds for all finitely supported
\mu,\nu
and both sides are continuous in
(\mu,\nu)
. Since, finitely supported measures are dense in
\mathcal{M}_{c}(-)
, this implies the claim.
∎

4.6.3.
The fundamental theorem of calculus

As mentioned before in
Remark

4.6.1.3
, one of the most prominent applications of vector-valued integration is infinite-dimensional calculus. To show that our setting fulfils a minimal requirement for such purposes, we provide a version of the fundamental theorem of calculus for curves in paired linear
hk
-spaces. We first need the following straightforward definition of a continuously differentiable curve in a paired linear
hk
-space.

Definition 4.6.3.1
.

Let
V
be a replete linear
hk
-space. A continuous curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to V
is said to be
continuously differentiable
if for all
t\in(0,1)
, the limit,

\gamma^{\prime}(t):=\frac{\gamma(t+h)-\gamma(t)}{h},

exists and the map
t\mapsto\gamma^{\prime}(t)
is continuous with a (necessarily unique) extension to a continuous curve
\gamma^{\prime}:[0,1]\to V
.

Proposition 4.6.3.2

(Fundamental theorem of calculus)
.

Let
V
be a paired linear
hk
-space and let
\gamma:[0,1]\to V
be a continuously differentiable curve. Then for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\int_{0}^{t}\gamma^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s:=\int^{V}\gamma^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda_{[0,t]}(s)=\gamma(t)-\gamma(0).

Conversely, let
\gamma:[0,1]\to V
be a continuous curve. Then

t\mapsto\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

is continuously differentiable and

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s=\gamma(t).

Proof.

For the first part, let
\lambda[a,b]\in\mathcal{M}([0,1])
be the Lebesgue measure restricted to the interval
[a,b]
(where
0<a<b<1
). Observe that for all
t\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\lambda{[t,t+h]}=\delta_{t}.

Using continuity of the integral, we see that for all
t\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\gamma^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}(\gamma(s+h)-\gamma(s))\Big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda[0,t](s)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\,\Big[\int\!\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{1}{h}\lambda{[t,t+h]}\Big)\>-\>\int\!\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{1}{h}\lambda{[0,h]}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\lambda{[t,t+h]}\Big)-\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\lambda{[0,h]}\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\delta_{t}(s)-\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\delta_{0}(s)=\gamma(t)-\gamma(0).

Continuity in
t
then yields the claim for all
t\in[0,1]
.

For the second part, we find that for all
t\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\Big(\int_{0}^{t+h}\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s-\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0}\int^{V}\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{1}{h}\lambda[t,t+h]\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Big(\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\lambda[t,t+h]\Big)

\displaystyle=\int\gamma(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\delta_{t}=\gamma(t).

As before, the claim then also follows for all
t\in[0,1]
.
∎

Remark 4.6.3.3
.

This proof is substantially different from the “usual” one concerning the setting of Fréchet spaces given in
[
Ham82
, p. 71, Theorem 2.1.1; p. 73f, Theorem 2.2.2, Theorem 2.2.3]
.

Chapter 5
Relation to Locally Convex Topological Vector Spaces

In this chapter, we will draw a detailed comparison between the setting of linear
k
-spaces and the classical theory of locally convex Hausdorff topological vector spaces (abbreviated LCTVS in the following). To this end, we will establish the following main points.

1.

Replete linear
hk
-spaces as
k
-ifications of LCTVS
: Replete linear
hk
-spaces are always the
k
-ification of some LCTVS (
5.1.1.2
). Moreover, as noted already by Frölicher and Jarchow
[
FJ72
]
, the category of those linear
k
-spaces which are the
k
-ification of some LCTVS is equivalent to a full subcategory of the category of LCTVS (
5.1.0.4
).

2.

Completeness of linear
k
-spaces:
The
k
-ification of an LCTVS is not always replete, and the missing condition to make this true is a completeness condition which we refer to as
k
-completeness
. We will show that a linear
hk
-space is replete if, and only if, it is both the
k
-ification of some LCTVS and
k
-complete (
5.2.3.6
). This is our contribution, complementing the findings of
[
FJ72
]
by providing an appropriate completeness condition for this context.

3.

Tensor products:
Fréchet spaces can be viewed as replete linear
hk
-spaces, as paired linear
hk
-spaces, or as complete LCTVS. Each of these interpretations comes with its own notion of tensor product. In the complete locally convex setting, the tensor product that satisfies the expected universal property is the
completed projective tensor product
. As turns out, for Fréchet spaces, the completed projective tensor product coincides with the tensor product as taken in the closed monoidal category of replete linear
hk
-spaces (
5.3.3.1
). As a consequence, it also coincides with the tensor product of paired linear
hk
-spaces.

5.1.
k
-Locally Convex Linear
k
-Spaces

Since our goal is to investigate the relationship between linear
k
-spaces and locally convex topological vector spaces, the following definition will be useful.

Definition 5.1.0.1

(
k
-Locally convex linear
k
-space)
.

1.

We call a linear
k
-space
V

k
-locally convex
if there is an LCTVS (i.e.
Hausdorff
locally convex topological vector space)
E
such that
V=kE
.

2.

Denote the category of LCTVS with continuous linear maps as morphisms by
\mathsf{LCTVS}
and write
k\mathsf{LCTVS}
for the full subcategory of the category
{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of linear
k
-spaces spanned by
k
-locally convex linear
k
-spaces.

Note that
k
-ification provides a functor,

k:\mathsf{LCTVS}\to k\mathsf{LCTVS}.

In the other direction, we can also define a functor moving from linear
k
-spaces to locally convex topological spaces.

Definition 5.1.0.2

(Associated LCTVS, compactly determined LCTVS)
.

1.

Given a linear
k
-space
V
, let
lV
be the LCTVS obtained by re-topologising
V
with the topological vector space topology generated by the convex
0
-neighbourhoods of
V
. We call
lV
the LCTVS
associated to

V
, or simply the
associated LCTVS
.

2.

Note that given a continuous linear map
f:V\to W
between linear
k
-spaces,
f
is also continuous when viewed as a map
lf
from
lV
to
lW
(since the preimage of a convex set under a linear map is convex).

3.

We denote the image of the resulting functor, restricted to
k\mathsf{LCTVS}
,

l:k\mathsf{LCTVS}\to\mathsf{LCTVS},

by
lk\mathsf{LCTVS}
. In other words,
lk\mathsf{LCTVS}
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{LCTVS}
spanned by spaces of the form
lV
for some
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space
V
.

4.

An LCTVS
E
will be called
compactly determined
if it can be written as
E=lkF
for some LCTVS
F
(i.e. if it is an object of
lk\mathsf{LCTVS}
). In other words, compactly determined LCTVS are exactly those LCTVS associated to some
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space.

Remark 5.1.0.3
.

The term “compactly determined” is due to Porta
[
Por72
]
. A detailed study of this class of LCTVS can be found there.

The precise relationship between
k
-locally convex linear
k
-spaces and compactly determined LCTVS was given in
[
FJ72
]
(and the same statement is given in
[
Sei79
, Theorem 2.2]
), which we record as:

Theorem 5.1.0.4
.

1.

The functor

l:k\mathsf{LCTVS}\to\mathsf{LCTVS}

is a left adjoint and a right inverse to

k:\mathsf{LCTVS}\to k\mathsf{LCTVS}.

2.

As a consequence, we have an equivalence of categories,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces lk\mathsf{LCTVS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k\mathsf{LCTVS},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

between the categories of
k
-locally convex linear
k
-spaces and compactly determined LCTVS.

3.

Moreover, the composite functor
lk:=l\circ k
,

lk:\mathsf{LCTVS}\to lk\mathsf{LCTVS},

is right adjoint to the inclusion functor

lk\mathsf{LCTVS}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{LCTVS},

exhibiting
lk\mathsf{LCTVS}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathsf{LCTVS}
.

5.1.1.
Replete linear
k
-spaces are
k
-locally convex

The following proposition ensures that most of the linear
k
-spaces we have encountered, in particular all replete linear
hk
-spaces, are
k
-locally convex.

Proposition 5.1.1.1
.

Let
V
be a
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space and let

\iota:W\hookrightarrow V

be a linear
k
-subspace. Then
W
is a
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space, as well.

Proof.

Let
E
be some LCTVS with
kE=V
(which there is by
k
-local convexity). Endow
W
with the subspace topology induced from
E
and call the resulting space
W_{LC}
. As a linear subspace of a LCTVS,
W_{LC}
is an LCTVS, as well, and we have an embedding

W_{LC}\hookrightarrow E,

whose
k
-ification is a
k
-embedding

kW_{LC}\hookrightarrow kE=V.

Hence, by our assumption that
\iota
is a
k
-embedding,
W=kW_{LC}
and therefore,
W
is
k
-locally convex, as claimed.
∎

Corollary 5.1.1.2
.

Let
V
be a replete linear
k
-space. Then
V
is
k
-locally convex.

Proof.

By definition, every replete linear
k
-space
V
admits a closed embedding into
V^{\wedge\wedge}\subseteq C(V^{\wedge})=kC_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(V^{\wedge})
, so the claim follows directly from
5.1.1.1
.
∎

5.1.2.
k
-Local convexity and the double dual

The following observation gives some evidence that what is missing from a
k
-locally convexity to imply repleteness is a kind of completeness condition. While repleteness means that the canonical map to the double dual is a
closed
embedding,
k
-local convexity amounts to this map being merely a
k
-embedding
[
FJ72
, Theorem 4.3]
:

Theorem 5.1.2.1
.

Let
V
be a linear
k
-space. Then
V
is
k
-locally convex if, and only, if

\eta^{V}:V\to V^{\wedge\wedge}

is a
k
-embedding.

5.2.
Completeness of Linear
k
-Spaces

5.2.1.
k
-Completeness

The notion of completeness that seems most appropriate in the context of linear
k
-spaces is the following.

Definition 5.2.1.1

(
k
-Completeness)
.

Let
V
be either a linear
k
-space, or a topological vector space (or any vector space carrying some topology).

1.

Recall that a
Cauchy net
is a net
(x_{i})_{i\in(I,\leq)}
in
V
such that for every neighbourhood
U
of the origin, there is some
i_{0}\in I
such that for all
i,j\geq i_{0}
, we have
x_{i}-x_{j}\in U
.

2.

A subset
S\subseteq V
is
totally bounded
if for every neighbourhood
U\subseteq V
of the origin, there exists a finite subset
F\subseteq V
such that
F+U\supseteq S
.

3.

A
totally bounded Cauchy net
is a Cauchy net
(x_{i})
in
V
such that
\{x_{i}\mid i\in I\}\subseteq V
is totally bounded.

4.

V
is
k
-complete
if every totally bounded Cauchy net in
V
converges.

5.

V
is
sequentially complete
if every Cauchy
sequence
in
V
converges.

We will show that a linear
k
-space is replete if, and only if, it is
k
-locally convex and
k
-complete (
5.2.3.6
).

Remark 5.2.1.2
.

In
[
Akb22
]
,
k
-complete LCTVS are referred to as
pseudo-complete
, but since this terms seems to have various other meanings, we have adopted an alternative, perhaps more informative terminology.

Observe that
k
-completeness implies sequential completeness:

Proposition 5.2.1.3
.

Let
V
be either a linear
k
-space, or a topological vector space. Then every Cauchy sequence in
V
is totally bounded. Hence, if
V
is
k
-complete, then it is sequentially complete.

Proof.

Let
(x_{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
V
and let
U\subseteq V
be a neighbourhood of the origin. Put

S:=\{x_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

We want to show that there is a finite subset
F\subseteq V
such that
F+U\supseteq S
. Since
(x_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence, there is some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n,m\geq n_{0}
, we have
x_{n}-x_{m}\in U
. In particular,
x_{n}\in x_{n_{0}}+U
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Hence, letting

F:=\{x_{n}\mid n\in\{0,\dots,n_{0}\}\},

we see that

S\subseteq\bigcup_{n\in\{0,\dots,n_{0}\}}x_{n}+U=F+U,

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

5.2.2.
Replete linear
k
-spaces are
k
-complete

We will now show that every replete linear
k
-space is
k
-complete (
5.2.2.7
). This will require a sequence of lemmas.

Lemma 5.2.2.1
.

Let
E
be an LCTVS and let
S\subseteq kE
be totally bounded in
kE
. Then
S
is totally bounded in
E
, as well.

Proof.

Let
U
be a neighbourhood of the origin in
E
. Then
U
is also a neighbourhood of the origin in
kE
and hence there is a finite subset
F\subseteq E
such that
F+U\supseteq S
, proving the claim.
∎

Lemma 5.2.2.2
.

Let
E
be an LCTVS and let
(x_{i})
be a Cauchy net in
kE
. Then
(x_{i})
is also a Cauchy net in
E
.

Proof.

U
be a neighbourhood of the origin in
E
. Then
U
is also a neighbourhood of the origin in
kE
and hence there is some
i_{0}\in I
such that for all
i,j\geq i_{0}
,
x_{i}-x_{j}\in U
, proving the claim.
∎

Lemma 5.2.2.3
.

Let
E
be a
k
-complete LCTVS and let
K\subseteq E
be closed and totally bounded. Then
K
is compact.

Proof.

In any LCTVS, a subset
S
is compact if, and only if, it is totally bounded and complete (meaning that every Cauchy net with members in
S
converges in
S
, see
[
Wil12
, p.262, Theorem 39.9]
). Since
K
is totally bounded, every Cauchy net in
K
is totally bounded and thus convergent by
k
-completeness of
E
. Hence,
K
is totally bounded and complete and therefore compact, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 5.2.2.4
.

Let
E
be a
k
-complete LCTVS. Then
V:=kE
is also
k
-complete.

Proof.

Let
(x_{i})
be a totally bounded Cauchy net in
V
and let

K:=\overline{\{x_{i}\mid i\in I\}}^{E}

be the closure of the set of its members in
E
. By
5.2.2.1
and the fact that the closure of a totally bounded set is totally bounded
[
Sch66
, p. 25, 5.1]
,
K
is totally bounded in
E
, and hence, by
k
-completeness of
E
and
5.2.2.3
, it is compact. By Lemmas
5.2.2.1
and
5.2.2.2
,
(x_{i})
is also a Cauchy net
E
. Using the
k
-completeness of
E
once more, we see that
(x_{i})
converges to some
x\in K
. Since the topologies of
E
and
V=kE
coincide on the compact subset
K
, and therefore,
(x_{i})
converges also in
V
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2.5
.

For any
k
-space
X
,
C(X)
is
k
-complete.

Proof.

C(X)=kC_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)
and
C_{\mathsf{c.o.}}(X)
is complete (since
X
is a
k
-space, see
[
Kel17
, p. 231, Theorem 12]
). The claim then follows from
5.2.2.4
.
∎

Proposition 5.2.2.6

(Closed subspaces inherit
k
-completeness)
.

Let
V
be a
k
-complete linear
k
-space and
W\subseteq V
be a closed linear subspace. Then
W
is
k
-complete, as well.

Proof.

Let
(x_{i})
be some totally bounded Cauchy net in
W
. Then, by
k
-completeness of
W
,
(x_{i})
converges to some
x\in V
and since
W\subseteq V
is closed,
x\in W
, completing the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.2.2.7
.

Every replete linear
k
-space is
k
-complete.

Proof.

Any replete linear
k
-space
V
can be identified with a closed subspace of
C(V^{\wedge})
, so the claim follows from combining
5.2.2.5
and
5.2.2.6
.
∎

5.2.3.
k
-Locally convex and
k
-complete linear
k
-spaces are replete

Complementing
5.2.2.7
, our next goal is to show that for
k
-locally convex linear
k
-spaces,
k
-completeness is also a sufficient condition for repleteness (
5.2.3.6
). Again, this will follow from a number of lemmas.

Lemma 5.2.3.1
.

Let
V
be a linear
k
-space and let
E:=lV
. Then:

1.

If
S\subseteq V
is totally bounded in
V
, then it is also totally bounded in
E
.

2.

If
(x_{i})
is a Cauchy net in
V
, then it is a Cauchy net in
E
, as well.

Proof.

1.

Let
U\subseteq E
be a neighbourhood of the origin in
E
. By the definition of
E=lV
,
U
contains a convex neighbourhood of the origin
C\subseteq V
in
V
. By total boundedness of
S
in
V
, there is a finite set
F\subseteq V
such that
F+C\supseteq S
. Hence,
F+U\supseteq S
and we see
S
is also totally bounded in
E
.

2.

Again, let
U\subseteq E
be a neighbourhood of the origin in
E
, which then
U
contains a convex neighbourhood of the origin
C\subseteq V
in
V
.
(x_{i})
being a Cauchy net in
V
, there is some
i_{0}
such that for all
i,j\geq i_{0}
,
x_{i}-x_{j}\in C\subseteq U
. Therefore,
(x_{i})
is a Cauchy net in
E
, as well.

∎

Corollary 5.2.3.2
.

Let
E
be a compactly determined LCTVS. Then:

1.

The totally bounded subsets and Cauchy nets of
E
and
kE
coincide.

2.

E
is
k
-complete if, and only if,
kE
is
k
-complete.

Proof.

This follows from combining the statements of Lemmas
5.2.3.1
,
5.2.2.2
,
5.2.2.1
and
5.2.2.4
.
∎

Lemma 5.2.3.3
.

Let
V=kE
be a
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space (with
E
some compactly determined LCTVS) and let
W\subseteq V
be a linear
k
-subspace. Suppose that
W
is
k
-complete and that
F:=lW\subseteq E
is an embedding, i.e. that
F
carries the subspace topology induced from
E
. Then
W
is closed in
V
.

Proof.

Let
K\subseteq V
be compact. Since
V
is a
k
-space, it suffices to show that
W\cap K
is closed in
K
. Let
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
be a net in
W\cap K
, converging in
K
. Now,
(x_{i})
is also convergent in
E
(since the topologies of
E
and
V=kE
coincide on compact subsets), and therefore a Cauchy net in
E
(since
in a topological vector space
, any convergent net is a Cauchy net). Moreover, being contained in a compact set, it is totally bounded. Since
F=lW
carries the subspace topology induced from
E
,
(x_{i})
is also a totally bounded Cauchy net in
F
, and by
5.2.3.2
,
(x_{i})
is then a totally bounded Cauchy net in
W
, as well. Hence, by
k
-completeness of
W
,
(x_{i})
converges to some
x\in W\cap K
, showing that
W\cap K\subseteq K
is closed, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Lemma 5.2.3.4
.

Let
V
be a linear
k
-space and let
U\subseteq V
be a neighbourhood of the origin. Then

K:=\{\phi\in V^{\wedge}\mid\,\forall x\in U:|\phi(x)|\leq 1\}\subseteq V^{\wedge}\subseteq C(V)

is compact.

Proof.

We apply the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem (
2.1.5.2
), for which we have to verify the following conditions:

1.

K\subseteq C(V)
is closed. (This is clear from the continuity of the evaluation map
\eta^{V}
, from which we see that
K
is an intersection of pre-images of a closed set.)

2.

The closure of
K(x):=\{\phi(x)\mid\,\phi\in K\}\subseteq\mathbb{K}
is compact, for each
x\in V
: let
x\in V
. Since
U
is a neighbourhood of the origin there exists a scalar
0\not=a\in\mathbb{K}
such that
ax\in U
. Now,
K(ax)=aK(x)
is bounded in
\mathbb{K}
, by definition of
K
, showing that the closure of
K(x)
is indeed compact.

3.

K
is equicontinuous: if
\epsilon>0
and
x_{0}\in V
, then for all
x\in x_{0}+(\epsilon/2)U
and
\phi\in K
,

|\phi(x)-\phi(x_{0})|<\epsilon.

∎

Lemma 5.2.3.5
.

Let
V
be a
k
-locally convex linear
k
-space. Then
l(\eta^{V}):lV\to l(V^{\wedge\wedge})
is an embedding.

Proof.

We know that
f:=l(\eta^{V})
is continuous and injective, so it suffices to show that it is open onto its image. Since every LCTVS has a basis of neighbourhoods of the origin consisting of closed absolutely convex sets, it is even sufficient to show that
f
maps such neighbourhoods to neighbourhoods in
f(lV)
. So let
C
be a closed absolutely convex neighbourhood of the origin in
lV
. The topology of
lV
being generated by the absolutely convex open subsets of
V
, we may suppose that
C
is a closed absolutely convex
0
-neighbourhood in
V
.

Consider

K:=\{\phi\in V^{\wedge}\mid\,\forall x\in C:|\phi(x)|\leq 1\}.

Then, by
5.2.3.4
,
K
is compact, and therefore,

U:=\{\eta^{V}(x)\in\eta^{V}(V)\mid\,\forall\phi\in K:|\eta(x)(\phi)|\leq 1\}

is a
0
-neighbourhood in
f(lV)
(since the topology of
f(lV)
, i.e. the subspace topology induced from
l(V^{\wedge\wedge})
, is finer than the compact-open topology, in which
U
is a basic open neighbourhood). Letting

{}^{\circ}K:=\{x\in V\mid\,\forall\phi\in K:|\phi(x)|\leq 1\}

(the “pre-polar”), we have that
U=f({}^{\circ}K)
. By the
bi-polar theorem
(see
[
Sch66
, p. 126]
),
{}^{\circ}K=C
and hence,
U=f(C)
, so the image of
C
is a
0
-neighbourhood in
f(lV)
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary 5.2.3.6
.

Let
V
be a linear
k
-space. Then
V
is replete if, and only if,
V
is
k
-locally convex and
k
-complete.

Proof.

From Corollaries
5.1.1.2
and
5.2.2.7
, we have that every replete linear
k
-space is
k
-locally convex and
k
-complete. On the other hand, if
V
is
k
-locally convex, then by
5.1.2.1
, the canonical evaluation map
\eta^{V}:V\to V^{\wedge\wedge}
is a
k
-embedding. Now, if
V
is furthermore
k
-complete, by Lemmas
5.2.3.3
and
5.2.3.5
, the image of
V
in
V^{\wedge\wedge}
under
\eta^{V}
is closed, showing that
V
is replete.
∎

Corollary 5.2.3.7
.

The
k
-ification of a
k
-complete locally convex topological vector space
E
is a replete linear
k
-space.

Proof.

The
k
-ification
kE
is
k
-complete by
5.2.3.2
and
k
-locally convex by definition. Hence, the claim follows from
5.2.3.6
.
∎

Corollary 5.2.3.8
.

The category of replete linear
k
-spaces is equivalent (even isomorphic) to that of
k
-complete, compactly determined LCTVS, via the mutually inverse functors
k
and
l
.

Proof.

This follows by combining
5.2.3.6
with the equivalence of the categories of
k
-locally convex linear
k
-spaces and compactly determined LCTVS (
5.1.0.4
), keeping in mind that
k
-completeness is preserved by the functors
k
and
l
that constitute the equivalence (see
5.2.3.2
).
∎

5.2.4.
Comparison to Pettis integral

We now compare the vector-valued integral from Chapter 4,
Section

4.6
, with the classical
Pettis integral

[
Pet38
]
.

Proposition 5.2.4.1
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure on a compact Hausdorff space
X
, let
E
be a
k
-complete LCTVS and let
f:X\to E
be a continuous map. By
5.2.3.7
,
kE
is a replete linear
hk
-space (and hence a paired linear
hk
-space, by letting
(kE)^{*}:=(kE)^{\wedge}
). Now, the vector valued integral (as defined in
4.6.1.1
),

I:=\int_{X}f(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

is the unique element
I\in E
satisfying that for all continuous linear functionals
\phi\in E
,

(5.1)

\phi(I)=\int_{X}\phi(f(x))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu(x),

where the left hand side denotes an ordinary Lebesgue integral.

Proof.

That
I
satisfies
Eq.

5.1
follows from
4.6.1.6
. That it is the unique element of
E
with this property follows from the fact that the dual of
E
separates points (since
E
is locally convex).
∎

Remark 5.2.4.2
.

The unique element
I\in E
is know as the
Pettis integral
or
weak integral
of
f
against
\mu
(see
[
Rud91
, p. 77, Definition 3.26]
). While its uniqueness follows directly from local convexity, as we have seen, its existence is non-trivial (see, for example,
[
Rud91
, p. 78, Theorem 3.27]
). Here, we have given an alternative way to show existence by passing to the setting of replete (or paired) linear
hk
-spaces and using the “monadic” construction from Chapter 4. As a consequence, the vector-valued integral from
Section

4.6
coincides with the classical Pettis integral (whenever the two are comparable).

5.2.5.
The sequential completion of locally convex Hausdorff topological vector spaces

Just as one can define the completion of an LCTVS, one can also construct its
sequential completion
satisfying an analogous universal property. We will need this construction in
Section

5.3
and recall it here for the convenience of the reader. Let us first give an explicit definition.

Definition 5.2.5.1

(Sequential completion)
.

Let
E
be an LCTVS and let
\overline{E}
be the completion of
E
(which is an LCTVS, see
[
Sch66
, p. 49, 4.1]
). Define the
sequential completion

\widehat{E}
to be the sequential closure (i.e. the smallest sequentially closed superset) of
E
in
\overline{E}
.

The sequential completion deserves this name for the reasons summarised in the following two propositions.

Proposition 5.2.5.2
.

The sequential completion
\widehat{E}
of an LCTVS
E
is sequentially complete.

Proof.

Let
(x_{n})
be a Cauchy sequence in
\widehat{E}
. Then
(x_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence also in
\overline{E}
and, by completeness of
\overline{E}
, it converges to some
x\in\overline{E}
. Since
\widehat{E}\subseteq\overline{E}
is sequentially closed,
x\in\widehat{E}
, so
(x_{n})
converges in
\widehat{E}
and
\widehat{E}
is sequentially complete.
∎

Proposition 5.2.5.3

(Universal property of the sequential completion)
.

Let
f:E\to F
be a continuous linear map, with
E
an LCTVS and
F
a sequentially complete LCTVS. Then there exists a unique continuous linear map,

\tilde{f}:\widehat{E}\to F,

making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute, where
E\hookrightarrow\widehat{E}\subseteq\overline{E}
is the inclusion map.

Proof.

By the universal property of the completion, there exists a unique continuous linear map
\overline{f}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

commute. Since
F
is sequentially complete and
\overline{f}
is continuous, the image of
\widehat{E}
(which is defined as a sequential closure) under
\overline{f}
is contained in
F
(which is sequentially closed). Hence, restricting
\overline{f}
to
\widehat{E}
yields the desired map
\tilde{f}:\widehat{E}\to F
.
∎

5.3.
Tensor Products

The purpose of this section is to compare the notions of tensor products of replete linear
hk
-spaces or paired linear
hk
-spaces with the classical
projective tensor product
of locally convex topological vector spaces. In particular, we will see that in the case of Fréchet spaces as well as in the dual case of Brauner spaces all of these notions coincide.

5.3.1.
The universal property of the tensor product of replete linear
hk
-spaces

In order to achieve our goal, we would like to formulate and compare universal properties of the different tensor products. We begin with the case of replete linear
hk
-spaces.

According to
3.3.2.12
, the category
r{\mathsf{Vect}(hk\mathsf{Top})}
of replete linear
hk
-spaces has a closed symmetric monoidal structure, with the tensor product given by the repletion of the tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces. Denote this tensor product by
\otimes_{r}
. Since the tensor product of linear
hk
-spaces comes equipped with a canonical map
V\times W\to V\otimes W
, composing with the canonical map
V\otimes W\to V\otimes_{r}W
to the repletion, we likewise have a canonical map
V\times V\to V\otimes_{r}W
. With this in mind, we can formulate the following universal property.

Proposition 5.3.1.1

(Universal property of repleted tensor product)
.

Let
V,W,Z
be replete linear
hk
-spaces and let
b:V\times W\to Z
be a continuous bilinear map. Then there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:V\otimes_{r}W\to Z
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\otimes_{r}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

Proof.

By the universal property of the tensor product
\otimes
of linear
hk
-spaces (which is a special case of the one for modules over a commutative monad, see
3.2.3.11
), there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}_{0}
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\otimes W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

commute. Applying the universal property of the repletion then yields the desired unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:V\otimes_{r}W\to Z
that makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\otimes_{r}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\otimes W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}

∎

5.3.2.
The sequentially completed projective tensor product of locally convex spaces

Recall that the
projective tensor product

E\otimes_{\pi}F
of two LCTVS
E
and
F
, which comes with a canonical continuous bilinear map
E\times_{\mathsf{Top}}F\to E\otimes_{\pi}F
, is characterised (up to unique isomorphism) by the following universal property (see
[
Kri16
, p. 33f]
, also for an explicit construction of
E\otimes_{\pi}F
). For any further LCTVS
G
and bilinear map
b:E\times_{\mathsf{Top}}F\to G
, there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:E\otimes_{\pi}F\to G
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\otimes_{\pi}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

commute.

Definition 5.3.2.1
.

Let
E,F
be
k
-complete LCTVS. The
sequentially completed projective tensor product
of
E
and
F
,

E\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}F:=(E\otimes_{\pi}F)\widehat{\;},

is the sequential completion (see
5.2.5.1
) of the projective tensor product .

Warning 5.3.2.2
.

The notation
\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}
is commonly used for the
completion
of the projective tensor product, which in general may be strictly larger than the sequential completion. However, within our context, this will not result in ambiguity, since we are interested in the case of Fréchet spaces, where the two notions agree by metrisability.

The sequentially completed projective tensor product satisfies the expected universal property.

Proposition 5.3.2.3
.

Let
E,F,G
be sequentially complete LCTVS and let
b:E\times F\to G
be a continuous bilinear map. Then there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:E\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}F\to G
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\>\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E\times F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

Proof.

Combine the universal property of the projective tensor product of LCTVS with that of the sequential completion.
∎

5.3.3.
The tensor product of replete linear
hk
-Spaces and the projective tensor product

Proposition 5.3.3.1
.

Let
V,W
be Fréchet spaces. Then the completed (equivalently, sequentially completed) projective tensor product
V\;\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W
agrees with the repleted tensor product
V\otimes_{r}W
of
V
and
W
.

Proof.

It suffices to verfify that
V\;\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W
satisfies the universal property of the tensor product of replete linear
hk
-spaces (see
5.3.1.1
). Let
Z
be some further replete linear
hk
-space and let
b:V\times W\to Z
be a continuous bilinear map. Since
Z
is replete, there exists a
k
-complete, compactly determined LCTVS
E
such that
Z=kE
(see
5.2.3.8
). In particular,
E
is sequentially complete (by
5.2.1.3
). Since
V
and
W
are metrisable,
V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}W
is a
k
-space and therefore,
b
is also continuous as a map
b:V\times_{\mathsf{Top}}W\to E
. By the universal property of the sequentially completed projective tensor product (see
5.3.2.3
), there is a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:V\;\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W\to E
making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

commute. But
V\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W
is a Fréchet space and hence a
k
-space, so a continuous map
V\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W\to E
is equivalently a continuous map
V\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W\to Z
. Therefore, there exists a unique continuous linear map
\tilde{b}:V\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W\to Z
(the same
\tilde{b}
as before) making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{V\times W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

commute, showing that
V\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W
does indeed satisfy the universal property of the tensor product of replete linear
hk
-spaces.
∎

Since the underlying linear
hk
-space of the tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}
of
paired
linear
hk
-spaces is exactly the tensor product of replete linear
hk
-spaces, we obtain the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 5.3.3.2
.

Let
V,W
be Fréchet spaces. Then their completed projective tensor product
V\;\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W
agrees with the paired-linear-
hk
-space tensor product of
V
an
W
. More precisely, let

\iota:\mathsf{Fre}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{L}

be the inclusion functor from the category of paired linear
hk
-spaces to the category of Fréchet spaces. Then

\iota(V\;\,\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}W)\cong\iota(V)\>\widehat{\otimes}\>\iota(W),

where the tensor product
\widehat{\otimes}
on the right hand side denotes the tensor product of paired linear
hk
-spaces.

,

,

and

.

Notation A.0.0.1
.

In the above diagram, the following notation is used.

1.

The “hooked” arrows
\hookrightarrow
denote fully faithful functors and the arrows

“
”

denote faithful functors.

2.

As customary, the “turnstile”
\uparrow\,\dashv\,\downarrow
means that the functor
\uparrow
is left adjoint to the functor
\downarrow
. Moreover, adjunctions decorated with a tensor product (“
\dashv\otimes
”) indicate symmetric monoidal adjunctions (see
3.2.2.3
). In particular, when the right adjoint is fully faithful, i.e. we have a reflective subcategory inclusion,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B},}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

of a symmetric monoidal closed category
\mathsf{A}
into a closed symmetric monoidal category, then this inclusion preserves internal homs. In this case it even holds that if
A\in\mathsf{A}
and
B\in\mathsf{B}
, then the internal hom
[A,B]
is in
\mathsf{B}
(i.e.
\mathsf{A}
is an
exponential ideal
in
\mathsf{B}
; this is Day’s reflection theorem, see Chapter 3,
3.2.4.12
). In each case when there is such a symmetric monoidal adjunction, this is either proved in the main body of this thesis, or given below in
A.0.0.3
.

Lemma A.0.0.2
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a cartesian closed category and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

be a reflective subcategory that is also cartesian closed. Assume furthermore that
\iota
is
dense
, meaning that the restricted Yoneda embedding

\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{D}},\;\;X\mapsto\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(-),X),

is fully faithful.

Then
\mathsf{D}
is an exponential ideal of
\mathsf{C}
, i.e. whenever
Y\in\mathsf{D}
and
X\in\mathsf{C}
, then
Y^{X}
is in
\mathsf{D}
(or, to be more precise,
\iota(X)^{Y}
is in the essential image of
\iota
).

Proof.

We first show that
\iota
preserves exponentials. Let
X,Y,Z\in\mathsf{D}
. Then, using that
\iota
preserves products (since it is a right adjoint),

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X),\iota(Z)^{\iota(Y)})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X)\times\iota(Y),\iota(Z))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X\times Y),\iota(Z))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(X\times Y,Z)

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(X,Z^{Y})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X),\iota(Z^{Y}))

Therefore, by our assumption that
\iota
is dense,

\iota(Z)^{\iota(Y)}\cong\iota(Z^{Y}).

Now, let
X,Z\in\mathsf{D}
,
Y\in\mathsf{C}
. We calculate,

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X),\iota(Z^{LY}))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(X,Z^{LY})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{D}}(LY,Z^{X})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(Y,\iota(Z^{X}))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(Y,\iota(Z)^{\iota(X)}))

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathsf{C}}(\iota(X),\iota(Z)^{Y})).

Hence, using our assumption once more,

\iota(Z)^{Y}\cong\iota(Z^{LY}).

In particular,
\iota(Z)^{Y}
is in the image of
\iota
, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Corollary A.0.0.3
.

The category
hk\mathsf{Top}
of
hk
-spaces (linear
hk
-spaces, resp.) is an exponential ideal in the category
\mathsf{Cond(Set)}_{q.s.}
of quasi-separated condensed sets (quasi-separated condensed vector spaces, resp.), and similarly, the category of quasi-separated condensed sets (quasi-separated condensed vector spaces, resp.) is an exponential ideal in the category
\mathsf{Cond(Set)}
of condensed sets (condensed vector spaces, resp.). In particular, the respective subcategory inclusions preserve all limits and internal homs.

Proof.

For the embedding of
hk
-spaces into (quasi-separated) condensed sets as a reflective subcategory, see
[
Sch19
, Proposition 1.7]
. For the fact that quasi-separated condensed sets form a reflective subcategory of condensed sets, see
[
SC20
, Lemma 4.14]
. In both cases, the inclusion is dense (since the respective subcategories contain all compact Hausdorff spaces), and
A.0.0.2
applies.

The reflective subcategory inclusion of linear
hk
-spaces into (quasi-separated) condensed vector space is also implied by
[
Sch19
, Proposition 1.7]
(see also
[
CS22
, p. 15]
; and similarly for the inclusion of quasi-separated condensed vector spaces into condensed vector spaces). Now, the claim follows from the fact that
hk
-spaces form an exponential ideal in condensed sets, and that the internal hom in the category of condensed vector spaces is formed from equalisers and internal homs in
\mathsf{Cond(Set)}
(cf. the proof of
2.2.2.3
in Chapter 2).
∎

Remark A.0.0.4
.

A.0.0.3
implies that our observations from Chapter 2 concerning the topology of the space of continuous linear maps
L(V,W)
between linear
hk
-spaces
V,W
can also be phrased as results concerning the internal hom of condensed vector spaces. For example, when
V
and
W
are separable Fréchet spaces,
2.2.3.3
implies that the internal hom
[\underline{V},\underline{W}]
of the associated condensed vector spaces
\underline{V},\underline{W}
is (the condensed vector space associated to)
L(V,W)
with the strong sequential topology. Similarly, questions concerning duality of linear
hk
-spaces can be phrased as questions about the duality of condensed vector spaces (see, for example,
1.4.3.6
). In particular, for any condensed vector space
V
, the internal hom
[V,\mathbb{K}]
(i.e. the “internal dual”) is (the condensed vector space associated to) a replete linear
hk
-space.
